The posets for all arcs in the resolution of an incompatibility in the
k=0
case.

If
k=0
or
k=m
, then for some
t\geq 1
, we have overlaps
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}[1,t]
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[1,t]
as in Definition
6
. Let
t
be the largest such
t
, so that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}(t+1)\neq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(t+1)
. Since we assume
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, such a value
t+1
must exist. Since these overlaps both begin at 1, these are not crossing overlaps

When
k=0
, the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
can be constructed in the same way as above, whereas
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
consists of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[>t]\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}[>t]
where we have the added relation
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)
based on the local configuration of these arcs. Similarly, if
k=m
, the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
follows from the
k\neq 0,m
case and the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
is given by
\mathcal{P}_{1}[>t]\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}[>t]
where we now have
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)
. See Table
3
for the
k=0
case; the
k=m
case is similar.

We can similarly deal with a singly-notched monogon
\gamma^{(p)}
, which is incompatible with itself by Definition
3
. If we follow the definition of
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{4}
now, we will instead get two closed curves. Again we will have that
\gamma_{3}
crosses
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}
and
\gamma_{4}
crosses
\sigma_{k+1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
. If the first and last triangles which
\gamma^{(p)}
passes through are the same, then we will be in the
k=0
case if the tagged endpoint of
\gamma
lies clockwise from the plain endpoint and otherwise we will be in the
k=m
case.

In the special case that
\gamma^{(p)}
is contractible, which is a subset of the
k=0
and
k=m
cases, we will have that one of the closed curves
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{4}
will be contractible and the other will be contractible onto
p
. Recall that for
\ell
contractible, in Definition
5
we set
x_{\ell}=-2
, and one can see from the band graph expansion formula that for
\ell
a closed curve contractible onto
p
,
x_{\ell}=1+Y_{p}
. We also introduce a definition for the element of the cluster algebra associated to a contractible singly-notched monogon.

Definition 7
.

Let
\gamma^{(p)}
be a contractible singly-notched monogon. Suppose the spokes from
T
at
p
are
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
. Then,

\displaystyle x_{\gamma^{(p)}}:=\begin{cases}Y_{p}-1&\textrm{if }k=0\\
1-Y_{p}&\textrm{if }k=m.\end{cases}

Comparing Definition
7
with Proposition
3
will show that this definition is compatible with our resolution for a non-contractible, singly-notched monogon.

Figure 5
:
A contractible singly-notched monogon in the
k=0
case (left) and the resulting contractible closed curves
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{4}

.

For the part of the following proposition concerning a pair of incompatible arcs, we assume the tagging of one endpoint of each arc. An advantage of our approach is that the tagging of the other endpoint of each arc does not affect the relation. Therefore, this proposition encompasses cases 6,7,9, and 11 from
[
6
]
. The case where
\gamma_{2}\in T
was also studied by Pilaud, Reading, and Schroll in
[
10
]
.

Proposition 3
.

Let
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and
\gamma_{2}
be arcs which are incompatible at a puncture
p
and let
\gamma^{(p)}
be a singly-notched non-contractible monogon.

1.

Suppose
\gamma_{2}=\sigma_{k}\in T
. Then,

x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}+(\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}^{\preceq\sigma_{k}}}y_{\tau})x_{\gamma_{4}}.

2.

Suppose
\gamma_{2}\notin T
. Let
t\geq 0
be the largest number such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}[1,t]
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}[1,t]
are overlaps, where in particular
t=0
if
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}(1)\neq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(1)
. Let
k
be as defined above. Set

Y_{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{S}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(-i+1)}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}y_{\sigma_{i}}

We have
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=X_{C^{+}}+X_{C^{-}}
where
X_{C^{+}}
and
X_{C^{-}}
are defined as follows.

X_{C^{+}}

X_{C^{-}}

k\neq 0,m

x_{\gamma_{3}}

Y_{S}x_{\gamma_{4}}

k=0

x_{\gamma_{4}}

Y_{R}x_{\gamma_{3}}

k=m

x_{\gamma_{3}}

Y_{S}Y_{R}x_{\gamma_{4}}

3.

Let
n=|\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}|
and let
t\geq 0
be the largest number such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[1,t]
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[d-t+1,d]
are self-overlaps, where in particular
t=0
if
\mathcal{P}(1)\neq\mathcal{P}(n)
. If we define
Y_{R},Y_{S},X_{C^{+}},
and
X_{C^{-}}
as above, then,
x_{\gamma^{(p)}}=X_{C^{+}}+X_{C^{-}}
.

Proof.

For shorthand in this proof, set
\mathcal{P}_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
and set
\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{P}_{3},\mathcal{P}_{4}
similarly.

Part 1)
\gamma_{2}=\sigma_{k}\in T

Every element in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
has at most one corresponding image in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
, so that under certain conditions we can consider subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{3}
or of
\mathcal{P}_{4}
. We define a map
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\to J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\cup J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
as

\Phi(I_{1})=\begin{cases}I_{1}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})&\sigma_{k}\notin I_{1}\\
I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle\in J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})&\sigma_{k}\in I_{1}\\
\end{cases}

This map is clearly a bijection.

Recall that, since
\gamma_{2}
is a plain arc in
T
,
\mathbf{g}_{2}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}
. We can quickly check that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}
. This is immediate if
k\neq 1
. If
k=1
and
a
is the smallest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
is maximal in
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}
, then our claim follows from noting that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
begins with
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)
, so that
\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}
contributes to
\mathbf{b}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
and to
\mathbf{r}_{\gamma_{3}}
.

Next, we check that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}^{\preceq\sigma_{k}}}\hat{y}_{\tau})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
.

If
k<m
, then by Corollary
1
we have

\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}^{\preceq\sigma_{k}}}\hat{y}_{\tau})=(\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{[k]}})-(\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}).

Given a statement
T
, let
\delta_{T}=1
if
X
is true and otherwise let
\delta_{T}=0
. We observe

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\mathbf{g}^{\prime}

and

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{[k]}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\mathbf{g}^{\prime}

where
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
refers to the contributions from
\mathcal{P}[>1]
. Our claim follows after noting
\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}-1=\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}
.

Now suppose
k=m
and
a
is the smallest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
is minimal in
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}
. Let
[\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)]
denote the third arc in the triangle formed by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)
. Then, using Lemma
2
and noting by Corollary
1
,
\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots\hat{y}_{\sigma_{m}})=0
, we have

\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}^{\preceq\sigma_{m}}}\hat{y}_{\tau})=(\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)})-(\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\mathbf{e}_{[\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)]}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}})

We can update
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
in this case with our additional knowledge of the first minimal element in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and again find
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}^{\preceq\sigma_{k}}}\hat{y}_{\tau})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
.

Part 2)
\gamma_{2}\notin T

If
k\neq 0,m
, then we set up the following map
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\to J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\cup J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})
,

\Phi((I_{1},I_{2}))=\begin{cases}I_{1}\cup I_{2}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})&\sigma_{k+1}\notin I_{1},\sigma_{k}\in I_{1}\text{ only if }\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{2}\\
I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle\cup I_{2}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

The conditions make it clear that our map constructs order ideals of both posets
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}
. Once we have verified that this map is well-defined, the fact that it is a bijection is immediate.

Now, we consider the special cases. First, let
k=0
; recall this means that the first triangles which
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and
\gamma_{2}
pass through are the same and that
\gamma_{2}
lies counterclockwise from
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
as on the lefthand side of Figure
4
. The relevant posets are given in Table
3
.

We partition
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup B
and define a map
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\to J({P}_{3})\cup J({P}_{4})
. Since
k=0
, we want a bijection
\Phi
which restricts to a weight-preserving bijection between
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
and such that for each
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
,
wt(I_{1})wt(I_{2})=\hat{Y}_{R}wt(\Phi(I_{1},I_{2}))
. Let
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{1}[1,t]
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{2}[1,t]
, and let
R_{3}
(
R_{4}
) denote the image of
R_{1}
(
R_{2}
) in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. Any elements outside
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
in
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
each have a unique image in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}
, so we will largely ignore these elements.

1.

Let
A_{1}
consist of all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{2}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{1}
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
, then
\Phi
acts by sending elements from
R_{1}
to
R_{3}
and from
R_{2}
to
R_{4}
. The image
\Phi(A_{1})
is all
I_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\in I_{4}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{4}
.

2.

Let
A_{2}
consist of all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{2}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{1}
; and, there exists a switching position between
I_{1}\cap R_{1}
and
I_{2}\cap R_{2}
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
, we know there exists at least one switching position between
I_{1}\cap R_{1}
and
I_{2}\cap R_{2}
. We map
I_{1}\cap R_{1}
and
I_{2}\cap R_{2}
to
R_{4}
and
R_{3}
respectively until the first switching position, and then we swap. The image
\Phi(A_{2})
is all
I_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\in I_{4}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{1}
; and, there is a switching position between
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
.

3.

Let
A_{3}
consist of all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{1}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)\notin I_{2}
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{3}
, we send
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
, and all other elements have clear images. The set
\Phi(A_{3})
is all
I_{4}\in\mathcal{P}_{4}
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{4}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
; and,
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{1}
.

4.

Let
B
consist of all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{1}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)\in I_{2}
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, we send
I_{1}\cup(I_{2}\backslash R_{2})
to the corresponding order ideal of
\mathcal{P}_{3}
.

Now that we understand the image of each subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, an inverse map could be quickly constructed.

The bijection for the case
k=m
is dual - one can swap
\sigma_{1}
with
\sigma_{m}
and the order of the conditionals to define a new partitioning
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup B
and proceed with similar maps.

When
k\neq 0,m
, the
\mathbf{g}
-vector calculations are similar to those for the case when
\gamma_{2}\in T
. Consider the case for
k=0
. Let
\mathbf{g}_{R}
be the contribution of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[2,t-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
. Notice this is equal to the contribution of
\mathcal{P}_{2}[2,t-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}
. Let
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
be the contribution of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[>t+1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}}
and define
\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}
similarly. We have that

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{R}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime},

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(t-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{R}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}.

By observing
\mathcal{P}_{4}
in Table
3
, we see
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
. Now, by Lemma
2
we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}-2\mathbf{g}_{R}.

Notice that for each term we have the opposite sign on
\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}=\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}
and
\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}=\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t)}
as in
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}
.
We conclude

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})=-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t+1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}.

Part 3) A singly-notched monogon

Given a singly-notched non-contractible monogon, one can use the same proofs as in the two arc case with only minor adjustments to reach the conclusion of part (3).

∎

Remark 6
.

In Example 3.16 of
[
9
]
, Mandel presents a similar result regarding singly-notched monogons in the context of coefficient-free cluster algebras. In this example, the approach involves introducing a covering space that covers the surface three times, referred to as a triple cover. This technique is applied alongside the digon relation, which concerns the interaction between a singly-notched arc and a plain arc, as discussed in
[
29
]
.

Remark 7
.

Since resolving incompatible taggings involves a different local move than resolving transverse crossings, the set
S
behaves slightly differently in this setting. However, one can notice that the spokes at
p
which contribute to the
y
-monomial are exactly those which lie counterclockwise of
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and clockwise of
\gamma_{2}
, mirroring the way arcs contribute to
S
from the description in the introduction.

In Proposition
3
, we assumed
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, and the resolution had two terms. However, if
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
, we can use similar techniques to prove a relation between
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
and
x_{\gamma_{2}}
.

Corollary 2
.

Let
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two arcs incompatible at a puncture
p
such that
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
. Let
\gamma_{3}=\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ_{CCW}\gamma_{2}
. Then,

x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}

Note that, up to orientation,
\gamma_{3}
is homotopic to
\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ_{CW}\gamma_{2}
. When
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are ordinary arcs, so that
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
and
x_{\gamma_{2}}
are cluster variables, this identity is known. Here, we can see this formula holds even for generalized arcs. This shows that our definition of the cluster algebra element
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p)}}
via the poset expansion formula is equivalent to a definition using such algebraic expressions. This equality was suggested in Section 8.2 of
[
6
]
. In the next section, we show a similar statement for doubly-notched arcs.

4.2
Incompatible at two punctures

Two items from the list of cases of skein relations in
[
6
]
concern a pair of arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
which are incompatible at two punctures. This pair could consist of two singly-notched arcs or a doubly-notched arc and a plain arc. In Section 3.2 of
[
43
]
it is shown that the modules corresponding to such a pair of arcs are related by Auslander-Reiten translation.

If
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, then we can use Proposition
3
twice to write an expression for
x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}}
in terms of arcs which do not have incompatibilities. This will result in an expression involving the following four closed curves. Suppose
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are incident to punctures
p
and
q
, and let
\mathsf{h}_{p}
and
\mathsf{h}_{q}
be small circles drawn around
p
and
q
respectively such that each does not intersect any arcs other than the spokes at the relevant puncture. Suppose both
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are oriented from
q
to
p
. These curves are also drawn in Figure
7
; compare with Figure
6
.

•

Let
\ell_{1}
denote the closed curve we have from beginning at the intersection point of
\gamma_{1}
and
\mathsf{h}_{q}
, following
\gamma_{1}
until its intersection point with
\mathsf{h}_{p}
, following
\mathsf{h}_{p}
in
clockwise
order until its intersection point with
\gamma_{2}
, following
\gamma_{2}^{-1}
until its intersection point with
\mathsf{h}_{q}
, and then following
\mathsf{h}_{q}
in
clockwise
order until we reach our starting point.

•

Let
\ell_{2}
be the closed curve obtained when we instead follow both
\mathsf{h}_{p}
and
\mathsf{h}_{q}
in counterclockwise order.

•

Let
\ell_{3}
be the closed curve obtained when we follow
\mathsf{h}_{p}
in clockwise order and
\mathsf{h}_{q}
in counterclockwise order.

•

Let
\ell_{4}
be the closed curve obtained when we follow both
\mathsf{h}_{p}
in counterclockwise order and
\mathsf{h}_{q}
in clockwise order.

If we have
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
, then we cannot use the methods from Section
4.1
since we assume
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
. We will show the results directly in this case. Note that
\ell_{2}
is contractible if
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
.

We assume the spokes at

We begin with the resolution of a doubly-notched arc and a plain arc that are incompatible at two punctures.

Proposition 4
.

If
\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}
and
\gamma_{2}
are doubly-notched and plain arcs respectively which are incompatible at both
p
and
q
and such that
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, then
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=Y_{1}x_{\ell_{1}}+Y_{2}x_{\ell_{2}}+Y_{3}x_{\ell_{3}}+Y_{4}x_{\ell_{4}}
where the tuple
(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3},Y_{4})
is given below.

k_{p}\neq 0,m

k_{p}=m

k_{p}=0

k_{q}\neq 0,h

(Y_{S^{p}},Y_{S^{q}_{1}},Y_{S^{p}}Y_{S^{q}_{1}},1)

(Y_{p}Y_{R^{p}},Y_{S^{q}_{1}},Y_{p}Y_{S^{q}_{1}}Y_{R^{p}},1)

(1,Y_{S^{q}_{1}}Y_{R^{p}},Y_{S^{q}_{1}},Y_{R^{p}})

k_{q}=h

(Y_{S^{p}},Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}},Y_{S^{p}}Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}},1)

(Y_{p}Y_{R^{p}},Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}},Y_{p}Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}}Y_{R^{p}},1)

(1,Y_{q}Y_{R^{p}}Y_{R^{q}},Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}},Y_{R^{p}})

k_{q}=0

(Y_{S^{p}}Y_{R^{q}},1,Y_{S^{p}},Y_{R^{q}})

(Y_{p}Y_{R^{p}}Y_{R^{q}},1,Y_{p}Y_{R^{p}},Y_{R^{q}})

(Y_{R^{q}},Y_{R^{p}},1,Y_{R^{p}}Y_{R^{q}})

The
y
-monomials in the above table are as follows, where
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
,

Y_{R^{p}}=\prod_{j=1}^{t_{p}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}\quad Y_{R^{q}}=\prod_{j=n_{1}-t_{q}}^{t_{q}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(j)}\quad Y_{S^{p}}=\prod_{j=1}^{k_{p}}y_{\sigma_{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{S^{q}_{1}}=\prod_{j=1}^{k_{q}}y_{\eta_{j}}

Now let
\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}
and
\gamma_{2}
be doubly-notched and plain arcs respectively which are incompatible at both
p
and
q
and such that
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
.
Let
Y_{R}=\prod_{j=1}^{n_{1}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(j)}
. Then, we have
x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\ell_{1}}+Y_{p}Y_{q}Y_{R}+Y_{R}
.

Proof.

If
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, then these follow from Proposition
3
. We illustrate one case. Suppose
k_{p}\neq 0,m
and and
k_{q}=h
. If we first resolve the incompatibility at
p
, from part (2) of Proposition
3
we have

x_{\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\gamma_{3}^{(q)}}+Y_{S^{p}}x_{\gamma_{4}^{(q)}}.

Now, we apply part (3) of Proposition
3
to
\gamma_{3}^{(q)}
and
\gamma_{4}^{(q)}
,

x_{\gamma_{3}^{(q)}}=x_{\ell_{4}}+Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}}x_{\ell_{2}}\qquad x_{\gamma_{4}^{(q)}}=x_{\ell_{1}}+Y_{q}Y_{R^{q}}x_{\ell_{3}},

and the claim follows.

If
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
, then we cannot use Proposition
3
. We will instead exhibit a bijection between
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\backslash\{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\emptyset),(\emptyset,\mathcal{P}_{2})\}
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{\ell})
and demonstrate a relation on the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors which will explain the relation.

We draw
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
below, where
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}
. We know
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}=\mathcal{P}_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{2}^{0}
, and we refer to
\mathcal{P}_{2}
as
R_{2}
for shorthand. We have two copies of this poset as a subset in
\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
, denoted
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
. We choose chronological orderings on the three posets so that its restrictions to
R_{1},R_{2},
and
R_{3}
all agree; consequentially, this ordering on
R_{4}
will be opposite, which we emphasize by writing
\overline{R_{4}}
.

\boxed{R_{1}}
\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{2}
\sigma_{m}
\vdots
\eta_{1}
\eta_{2}
\eta_{h}
\vdots

\boxed{R_{2}}

R_{3}
\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{2}
\sigma_{m}
\vdots
\eta_{1}
\eta_{2}
\eta_{h}
\vdots
\boxed{\overline{R_{4}}}

\mathcal{P}_{1}

\mathcal{P}_{2}

\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}

Let
A_{1}
denote all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that there is a switching position between
I_{1}|_{R_{1}}
and
I_{2}=I_{2}|_{R_{2}}
. We define the map
\Phi
on
A_{1}
by sending
I_{1}|_{R_{1}}
to
R_{3}
and
I_{2}
to
R_{4}
first and then swapping at the switching position. The
\sigma_{i}
and
\eta_{i}
will always be sent to their respective images in
\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
. The set
\Phi(A_{1})
is all
I\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
such that there is a switching position between
I|_{R_{3}}
and
I|_{R_{4}}
.

Let
A_{2}
denote all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
I_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
I_{1}|_{R_{1}}=\emptyset
but
I_{1}\neq\emptyset
. If
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
,
\Phi
acts on
(I_{1},I_{2})
by sending
R_{2}
to
R_{4}
, and otherwise
\Phi
acts by sending
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
. The set
\Phi(A_{2})
consists of
I\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
such that (
I|_{R_{3}}=\emptyset
;
I|_{R_{4}}=R_{4}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\notin I
) or (
I|_{R_{4}}=\emptyset
;
I|_{R_{3}}=R_{3}
; and,
\eta_{1}\notin I
).

Let
A_{3}
denote all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
I_{2}=\emptyset
and
I_{1}|_{R_{1}}=R_{1}
but
I_{1}\neq\mathcal{P}_{1}
. If
\sigma_{m}\in I_{1}
,
\Phi
acts on
(I_{1},I_{2})
by sending
R_{1}
to
R_{4}
and otherwise
\Phi
acts on
(I_{1},I_{2})
by sending
R_{1}
to
R_{3}
. The set
\Phi(A_{2})
consists of
I\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
such that (
I|_{R_{3}}=\emptyset
;
I|_{R_{4}}=R_{4}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\in I
) or (
I|_{R_{4}}=\emptyset
;
I|_{R_{3}}=R_{3}
; and,
\eta_{1}\in I
).

We see
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
is a partitioning of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\backslash\{(\mathcal{P}_{1},\emptyset),(\emptyset,\mathcal{P}_{2})\}
and
\Phi(A_{1})\cup\Phi(A_{2})\cup\Phi(A_{3})
is a partitioning of
\mathcal{P}_{\ell_{1}}
.

Now, our final step is to show
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{p}\hat{Y}_{q})=\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})=0
where
\mathbf{g}_{1}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(p,q)}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{2}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}
. The first equality follows immediately from Corollary
1
.

Let
\mathbf{g}_{R}
denote the contributions of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[2,n_{1}-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1}
, which is equivalent to the contributions of
\mathcal{P}_{2}[2,n_{2}-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{2}

We compute

\mathbf{g}_{1}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1})\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1})}+\mathbf{g}_{R},

and

\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1})\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1}-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n_{1})}+\mathbf{g}_{R}.

Now, Lemma
2
guarantees
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=-\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})
.
∎

We note that when
k_{p}=m
,
Y_{S^{p}}=Y_{p}
and similarly for
k_{q}=h
.

Proposition 5
.

\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and

and

k_{p}\neq 0,m

(Y_{S^{p}}Y_{S^{q}_{2}},1,Y_{S^{p}},Y_{S^{q}_{2}})

(Y_{S^{p}},Y_{R^{q}},Y_{S^{p}}Y_{R^{q}},1)

(1,Y_{R^{p}}Y_{R^{q}},Y_{R^{q}},Y_{R^{p}})

-monomials

and

.

If
\gamma_{1}^{(p)}
and

\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}

.

Again, if
\gamma_{1}^{0}\neq\gamma_{2}^{0}
, Proposition
5
follows from Proposition

. The case
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
can be shown in a similar manner as this case in Proposition

.

Each entry in the table in Proposition

results from taking the corresponding entry in the table in Proposition

, swapping the entries

and
Y_{3}
and the entries

and

with

where necessary.

By combining Propositions
4
and
5
in the case where
\gamma_{1}^{0}=\gamma_{2}^{0}
, we show a generalization of Theorem 12.9 from
[
3
]
for arcs which possibly have self-intersections. Recently, similar relations were studied in
[
44
]
with the goal of comparing various frieze patterns arising from twice-punctured discs.

Corollary 3
.

Let
\gamma
be a (possibly generalized) plain arc incident to two punctures
p
and
q
. Let
Y_{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{1}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}
. Then,

x_{\gamma}x_{\gamma^{(p,q)}}-x_{\gamma^{(p)}}x_{\gamma^{(q)}}=(1-Y_{p})(1-Y_{q})Y_{R}.

5
Transverse Crossings

Here we consider two tagged arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
whose interiors have at least one point of intersection. As discussed in Section
3.5
and in
[
23
]
, there are several ways this intersection can appear. We refer to these as intersections of Type 0, Type 1, and Type 2, where an intersection of Type
i
occurs if the intersection point occurs in the first or last triangle passed through by
i
of the two arcs. An intersection of Type 0 is exactly the type of intersection which produces crossing overlaps in the posets
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}
.

Type 0

Type 1

Type 2

Our first three subsections deal with, in order, intersections of Type 0,1, and 2, with the assumption that none of the arcs involved are in
T
nor any version of these arcs’ notchings removed. The last subsection is devoted to an intersection between two arcs where the plain underlying version of one arc is in the triangulation.

Notice that this sorting of cases differs from the sorting in Section 8 of
[
6
]
. For example, in Section
5.1
, we work with Type 0 intersections and all possible options for notchings on the endpoints of the two arcs involved.

5.1
Type 0

Consider two arcs,
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
not in
T
and suppose that the posets
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}
have a crossing overlap, so that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
cross and near this point of intersection, the two arcs cross the same set of arcs from
T
. The resolution consists of
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
where
\gamma_{3}:=\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}
,
\gamma_{4}:=\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
,
\gamma_{5}:=\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
, and
\gamma_{6}:=\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{2}
, where now the notation
\circ
refers to switching between the two arcs at their point of intersection.

Figure 8
:
A type
0
intersection in the case of
s>1,s^{\prime}>1,t<n_{1}=|\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}|,t^{\prime}<n_{2}=|\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}|
.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be indexed so that, if their crossing overlaps are
R_{1}=\{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(s),\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(t)\}
and
R_{2}=\{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(s^{\prime}),\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(t^{\prime})\}
, then
R_{1}
is on the top of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
and
R_{2}
is on the bottom of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}
.
Then,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}[\leq t]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[>t^{\prime}]
where from the geometry of the triangulation we have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(t^{\prime}+1)
. Similarly,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[\leq t^{\prime}]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}[>t]
where we also know
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(t)
.

There will be several cases for
\mathcal{P}_{5}
based on whether
s=1
or
s^{\prime}=1
and similarly whether
t=n_{1}
or
t^{\prime}=n_{2}
. Recall we cannot have
s=s^{\prime}=1
nor
t=n_{1}
and
t^{\prime}=n_{2}
simultaneously. Let
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}[<s]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[<s^{\prime}]
where we know from
T
that we also have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(s-1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(s^{\prime}-1)
. A chronological ordering on
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}
would have to reverse the ordering on either the component from
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
or the component from
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}
. Throughout, let
\alpha
be the counterclockwise neighbor of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(1)
and let
\beta
be the clockwise neighbor of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(1)
.

1.

If
s>1
and
s^{\prime}>1
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}
.

2.

If
s=1
, and
s(\gamma_{1})
is plain, then we have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}
. There are two special cases where we introduce a decoration.

(a)

If this set is empty, implying that
s(\gamma_{2})
is plain, then we include decoration
\alpha
.

(b)

If this set consists entirely of elements from a loop in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}
, implying that
s(\gamma_{2})
is notched, then we include decoration
\alpha^{-}
.

3.

If
s=1
and
s(\gamma_{1})
is notched, then we again have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}
. We explain which relations will connect the loop at the beginning of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
with the rest of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
.

(a)

If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[<s^{\prime}]\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
will be the poset for
\alpha^{(s(\gamma_{1}))}
, and thus must have decoration
\alpha^{-}
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[<s^{\prime}]\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}
consists only of elements of a loop, then
\mathcal{P}_{5}
will be the poset for
\alpha^{(s(\gamma_{1}),s(\gamma_{2}))}
and thus we do not include a decoration.

(c)

If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[<s^{\prime}]\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}
contains elements not in a loop, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
will be the result of attaching the elements of the loop from
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
onto the element of this set with largest index in the chronological ordering.

4.

If
s^{\prime}=1
instead, then we have
parallel cases, using instead
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\succeq(s-1)}
and
\beta
.

If
t=n_{1}
or
t=n_{2}
, we perform an identical operation, beginning with
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}[>t]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[>t^{\prime}]
. If
t=n_{1}
, then we remove
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}^{\preceq(t^{\prime}+1)}
and if
t^{\prime}=n_{2}
, then we remove
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}^{\succeq(t+1)}
; in each case, we have the same subcases. We highlight that in Case iii of the following proof we introduce a method of indexing a chain of spokes which will be used repeatedly in later proofs. This indexing is depicted in Figure
9
.

Proposition 6
.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two arcs with a Type 0 crossing, indexed so that the crossing overlap
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}[s,t]
is on top and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
is on bottom. Let the resolution be
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
. Then,

x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{R}Y_{S_{s}}Y_{S_{t}}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}

where

Y_{R}=\prod_{i=s}^{t}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(i)},

Y_{S_{s}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(i)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(s-1)\\
i\leq s-1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(i)}&s^{\prime}=1\text{ and }s(\gamma_{2})\text{ is notched}\\
\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(i)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(s^{\prime}-1)\\
i\leq s^{\prime}-1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(i)}&s=1\text{ and }s(\gamma_{1})\text{ is plain}\\
1&\text{ otherwise,}\\
\end{cases}

and

Y_{S_{t}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(i)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(t+1)\\
i\geq t+1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(i)}&t^{\prime}=n_{2}\text{ and }t(\gamma_{2})\text{ is notched}\\
\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(i)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(t^{\prime}+1)\\
i\geq t^{\prime}+1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(i)}&t=n_{1}\text{ and }t(\gamma_{1})\text{ is plain}\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

Since the definition of the posets
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}
vary based on the notchings of the endpoints of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
and whether these endpoints are near the overlap, we will break our proof into cases.

We set
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
throughout the proof. Let
R_{3}
denote the image of
R_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and define
R_{4}
similarly.

Case i)
s>1
;
s^{\prime}>1
;
t<n_{1}
; and,
t^{\prime}<n_{2}
.

The notching of each arc does not affect this case, so here the result follows from the plain case as in
[
23
,
7
]
. However, we will write the details in our setting of order ideals as they will be used in later cases where the notching does have an effect. We proceed with the usual strategy, providing a partition of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
and defining
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\to(J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4}))\cup(J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6}))
.

\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)
\boxed{R_{1}}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)

\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)
\boxed{R_{2}}
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)

\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)
\boxed{R_{3}}
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)

\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)
\boxed{R_{4}}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)

1.

Let
A_{1}
denote the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that there is a switching position between
I_{1}|_{R_{1}}
and
I_{2}|_{R_{2}}
. Given such a pair, we define
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})
to send
R_{1}
to
R_{3}
and
R_{2}
to
R_{4}
until the first switching position and then swap. The image of other elements is clear. The set
\Phi(A_{1})
would be all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that there is a switching position between
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
.

2.

Let
A_{2}
denote the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
R_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
and
R_{2}\cap I_{2}=\emptyset
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
, we define
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})
to send
R_{1}
to
R_{3}
. The image of
A_{2}
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\notin I_{3}
.

3.

Let
A_{3}
denote the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{1}\cap R_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\notin I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{3}
, the map
\Phi
acts by sending
R_{2}
to
R_{4}
. The set
\Phi(A_{3})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
and
R_{4}\subseteq I_{4}
.

4.

Let
A_{4}
denote the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{1}\cap R_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)\in I_{1}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)\notin I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{3}
, the map
\Phi
acts by sending
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
. The set
\Phi(A_{4})
is all all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
.

5.

Let
B
denote the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{1}\cap R_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)\in I_{2}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, the set
I_{1}\cup(I_{2}\backslash R_{2})
can be naturally partitioned into an element of
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
, and this map is bijective.

We omit the proof for
\mathbf{g}
-vectors as this case is already known.

Case ii) (
s=1
;
s(\gamma_{1})
is plain;
t<n_{1}
; and,
t^{\prime}<n_{2}
) or
(
s^{\prime}=1
;
s(\gamma_{2})
is plain;
t<n_{1}
; and,
t^{\prime}<n_{2}
).

The remaining notching of the arcs does not affect the combinatorics for these cases, so these follow from previously known results, such as from
[
23
,
7
]
.

Case iii)
s=1
;
s(\gamma_{1})
is notched;
t<n_{1}
; and,
t^{\prime}<n_{2}
.

Here, we introduce some notation which will be used repeatedly in later proofs. Let
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
denote the spokes at
s(\gamma_{1})
, indexed in counterclockwise order, such that the first triangle
\gamma_{1}
passes through is bound by
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{m}
. Then, before
\gamma_{2}
starts crossing the arcs recorded in the region
R_{2}
, it will cross some of these spokes; in particular,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}-1)=\sigma_{m}
.
\gamma_{2}
may wind around
s(\gamma_{1})
so that it could cross some spokes multiple times. This creates a chain, which we index
\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\succ\sigma_{m-1}^{(w)}\succ\cdots\succ\sigma_{1}^{(w)}\succ\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\succ\cdots\succ\sigma_{k}^{(1)}
for some
w\geq 1
and
k\in[m]
. We assume that this is the maximal, saturated chain of these spokes so that if
\gamma_{2}
crosses an arc before this, it is not a spoke at
s(\gamma)
. Let
\mu
denote the element before (with respect to the chronological ordering)
\sigma_{k}^{(1)}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, if it exists. We will assume we are in situation 3.b or 3.c in the description before the statement; situation 3.a is simpler so we do not provide the details.

We draw
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2},\mathcal{P}_{5},
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
in Figure
9
. The posets
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}
come from taking
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
respectively and swapping
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)
.

\boxed{R_{1}}
\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{2}
\sigma_{m}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)
\vdots
\cdots

\cdots
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)
\boxed{R_{2}}
\sigma_{m}^{(w)}
\sigma_{m-1}^{(w)}
\vdots
\sigma_{1}^{(w)}
\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
\vdots
\sigma_{k}^{(1)}
\mu
\cdots

\cdots
\mu
\sigma_{k}
\sigma_{k+1}
\sigma_{k-1}
\vdots

\cdots
\cdots
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)

\mathcal{P}_{1}

\mathcal{P}_{2}

\mathcal{P}_{5}

\mathcal{P}_{6}

Figure 9
:
These posets are used in the proof of Proposition
6
. The posets
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
showcase an indexing system which we use frequently in later sections.

The sets
A_{1},A_{2},
and
A_{3}
and the definition of
\Phi
restricted to each is identical to the general case. Thus, we have that the complement of
\Phi(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})
in
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
consists of pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3},R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
, and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
. We partition the remainder of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
below and complete the definition of
\Phi
.

Given an arbitrary element
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, let
\sigma_{x}
denote the maximal element of
I_{1}
in the loop and let
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
denote the maximal element of
I_{2}
on the chain of spokes. It is of course possible one or both of these do not exist. We will use these symbols in the same way when referring to
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
.

4.

Let
A_{4}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)=\sigma_{1}\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{4}
, we define
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})
as the map which sends
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
, and the elements in the loop and chain of spokes to the respective elements in
I_{3}
and
I_{4}
respectively. The set
\Phi(A_{4})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
; and,
x<m
.

5.

Let
A_{5}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
; and,
x\geq k-1
with strict inequality if
\mu\in I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{5}
, we define
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})
as the map which sends
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
and which sends
(\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\rangle)
to
(\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(A_{5})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
; and,
x=m
.

6.

Let
A_{6}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
; and,
z>1
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{6}
, we define
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})
as the map which sends
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
and which sends
(\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\rangle)
to
(\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle)
where we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}:=\sigma_{m}^{(z-1)}
if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist. The set
\Phi(A_{6})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
.

7.

Let
B
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
;
x\leq k-1
;
z=1
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\in I_{2}
; and,
\sigma_{k-1}\in I_{1}
only if
\mu\in I_{2}
. If
\mu
does not exist, we ignore the last criterion. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, there is a natural way to break
I_{1}\cup(I_{2}\backslash R_{2})
into a pair of elements of
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
, and this describes a bijective map.

For our computations relating to
\mathbf{g}
-vectors, we assume
\mu
exists( equivalently,
\gamma_{5}^{0}\notin T
). If this was not the case, the proof would be similar but would use the idea of a decorated poset.

We begin by listing
\mathbf{g}_{1}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{1}^{(s(\gamma))}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{2}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{2}}
. Let
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
refer to the contributions of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[>t+1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1}
,
\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}
refer to the contribution of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
before
\sigma_{k}^{(1)}
to
\mathbf{g}_{2}
,
\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
refer to the contribution of
\mathcal{P}_{2}[>t^{\prime}+1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{2}
, and
\mathbf{g}_{R}
refer to the contribution of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{1}[s+1,t-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1}
(equivalently of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{2}[s^{\prime}+1,t^{\prime}-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{2}
). Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{1}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{R},

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime})\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime}+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime})}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})}

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{R}.

We require
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)
to exist in order for “
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)
” to be true.

Setting
\mathbf{g}_{i}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{i}}
for remaining values of
i
, one can observe that

\mathbf{g}_{3}=\mathbf{g}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}-(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}-\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}

and similarly

\mathbf{g}_{4}=\mathbf{g}_{2}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}-\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}

and since
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})
correspond to the same arc in
T
, we conclude
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{g}_{3}+\mathbf{g}_{4}
.

Now, we have
\mathbf{g}_{5}=\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{6}=(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+2)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
. By Lemma
2
, we have

\displaystyle\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(2)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}+\mathbf{g}_{R},

and after noting that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(1)
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(s^{\prime})
also correspond to the same arc in
T
, we conclude
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R})=\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}
.

Case iv)
s^{\prime}=1
;
s(\gamma_{2})
is notched;
t<n_{1}
; and,
t^{\prime}<n_{2}
.

We use a similar indexing as in Case iii. Let the spokes at
s(\gamma_{2})
again be
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
with the same convention. Here,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-1)
corresponds to
\sigma_{1}
. The chain of spokes immediately before
R_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is of the form
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\prec\sigma_{2}^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\prec\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{k}^{(w)}
for some
w\geq 1
and
k\in[m]
. Let
\rho\in T
correspond to the element immediately before
\sigma_{k}^{(w)}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, if it exists. As in previous cases, we let the restriction of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
to the chain of spokes and loop in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
be
\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle
and
\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
respectively. If
I_{1}
is not supported on the chain of spokes, it will be convenient to set
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}:=\sigma_{m}^{(0)}
.

We again use the same sets
A_{1},A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
and the definition of
\Phi
as in the previous case. We will define 4 more sets
A_{4},A_{5},A_{6}
, and
B
. We define these such that
A_{4}\cup A_{5}\cup A_{6}\cup B
consists of all sets such that
R_{1}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
;
R_{2}\subseteq I_{2}
; and,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
. For all three new sets, our bijection will send
R_{2}
to
R_{3}
and will send elements outside
R_{i}
, the loop, and the chain of spokes to their corresponding elements in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
or
\mathcal{P}_{4}
.

4.

Let
A_{4}
consist of pairs satisfying the above condition and such that
y\neq m
and if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succ\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
, then
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{k}
with equality only if
\rho\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{4}
,
\Phi
sends
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
to
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)
. As before, we interpret
\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle=\langle\sigma_{m}^{(z-1)}\rangle
if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist. The set
\Phi(A_{4})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{4}
; and, if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
, with equality only if
\rho\in I_{3}
.

5.

Let
A_{5}
consist of pairs satisfying the above condition and such that
y=m
(including
\sigma_{m}^{(0)}
) and if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
with equality only if
\rho\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{5}
,
\Phi
sends
(\langle\sigma_{m}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
to
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z+1)}\rangle,\emptyset)
. The set
\Phi(A_{5})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
; and,
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{4}
.

6.

Let
A_{6}
consist of pairs satisfying the above condition and such that
z=w
and
x\geq k
with equality only if
\rho\notin I_{3}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{6}
,
\Phi
sends
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
to
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(A_{6})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
R_{3}\subseteq I_{3}
;
R_{4}\cap I_{4}=\emptyset
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{4}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{3}
;
z=w
; and,
x\geq k
with equality only if
\rho\notin I_{3}
.

7.

Let
B
be the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
;
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{k}
;
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\in I_{1}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\in I_{2}
;
\rho\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{k+1}\in I_{2}
; and,
\sigma_{1}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
. We interpret
\langle\sigma_{m}^{(0)}\rangle=\emptyset
, and if
k=m
, the condition “
\rho\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{k+1}\in I_{2}
” is replaced with “
\rho\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{1}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
”. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, the set
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash(\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle\cup R_{2}))
can naturally be partitioned into elements of
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
, and this forms a bijective map.

We turn to the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors, focusing on the case where
\rho
exists and
\sigma_{k}^{(w)}\neq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}
; one can make small adjustments if one or both of these statements is/are not true. Let our specific element
\rho
from be poset element
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
. Let
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
concern the contributions of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[<b]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1}
, and define
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathbf{g}_{R}
as in the previous case. In particular, we do not have a term
\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}
as there is no portion of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
before the region we are focusing on. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{1}=(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(b-1)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(s)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(s+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(s)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+2)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{R}

and

\mathbf{g}_{2}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})\prec\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime})}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{R}.

Showing
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{g}_{3}+\mathbf{g}_{4}
is akin to the previous case. We turn to comparing
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}
with
\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}
. Recall the monomial
Y_{S_{s}}
is not just 1 in this case, so we again use Lemma
2
to compute its
x
-degree vector,

\displaystyle\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{S_{s}})

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t+1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(t^{\prime}+1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+2\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-2\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(s)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(s+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(s)}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)}-2\mathbf{g}_{R}.

Now, similar to the previous case, we have
\mathbf{g}_{5}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(b-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{g}_{6}
is identical to the previous case. We can conclude
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{S_{s}})=\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}
.

All other cases)

If we are in the case where either
t=n_{1}
or
t^{\prime}=n_{2}
and
s,s^{\prime}
are both larger than 1, we can switch the chronological ordering of both posets and use the previous cases.

If we are in a case where
s
or
s^{\prime}
is 1 and
t=n_{1}
or
t^{\prime}=n_{2}
, we can use the ideas from the previous cases in two different ways to construct similar bijections with a finer partitioning of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
.

∎

5.2
Type 1

Here we consider an intersection between two arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
which occurs before the first crossing between
\gamma_{2}
and
T
or dually after the last crossing but between the first crossing and last crossings of
\gamma_{1}
and
T
. As a convention, we will choose an orientation of
\gamma_{2}
so that the intersection is before the first crossing of
\gamma_{2}
and
T
. We will assume
s(\gamma_{2})
is notched; if this is not the case, then the skein relation and proofs will be the same as in the unpunctured case. An example is shown in Figure
10
.

\sigma_{1}
\sigma_{m}
\sigma_{\ell+1}
\sigma_{\ell}
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)
\sigma_{k-1}
\sigma_{k}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
\gamma_{2}
\bowtie
\gamma_{1}

Figure 10
:
An illustration of the type of intersections we study in Section
5.2
as well as the notation we will use. We can have
k\geq\ell
as well and it is possible that
\gamma_{1}
does not intersect the arcs labeled
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
.

Let
p=s(\gamma_{2})
, and suppose the spokes in
T
which are incident to
p
are
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
, indexed in counterclockwise order and such that the first triangle that
\gamma_{2}
passes through is bounded by
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{m}
. Since
\gamma_{1}
crosses
\gamma_{2}
in a Type 1 intersection,
\gamma_{1}
must cross a consecutive sequence of at least two spokes incident to
p
, which, up to the choice of orientation, is of the form
\sigma_{k},\ldots,\sigma_{m},\sigma_{1},\ldots\sigma_{\ell}
, for some
1\leq k,\ell\leq m
.

It is possible that
\gamma_{1}
winds around this puncture multiple times, thus crossing
\gamma_{2}
in multiple Type 1 intersections. Let
w\geq 2
be such that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
intersect
w-1
times in the first triangle
\gamma_{2}
passes through. We let
a<b
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(b)
correspond to the arcs from
T
which
\gamma_{1}
crosses immediately before and after this sequence of crossings with spokes at
p
respectively. However, these values might not exist.

We will use indexing for
\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}
similar to that depicted in Figure
9
. So the sequence of spokes crossed will produce a chain

\sigma_{k}^{(1)}\prec\sigma_{k+1}^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\prec\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{\ell}^{(w)}.

We also can see from Figure
10
that
\sigma_{k}^{(1)}\prec\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\sigma_{\ell}^{(w)}\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
.

Label the Type 1 intersection points between
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
as
1,2,\ldots,w-1
, which are ordered with respect to the orientation of
\gamma_{1}
. The resolution of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
at a given intersection point consists of two sets of size 2,
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
. We index these so that
\gamma_{3}
is the result of following
\gamma_{1}
with opposite orientation from
t(\gamma_{1})
to the chosen intersection point and then following
\gamma_{2}
with positive orientation, which we denote
\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{2}
. Then,
\gamma_{4}=\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
,
\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{6}=\gamma_{1}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
. The arcs
\gamma_{4}
and
\gamma_{6}
are homotopic to arcs which do not cross any spokes since they each have an endpoint at
p
. The posets for these arcs are depicted in Table
4
, whereas before we set
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
.

Proposition 7
.

\gamma_{1}

x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=Y_{\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(r)}}x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}

Y_{\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(r)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(r)}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}\qquad Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(r+1)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(r+1)}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}.

:
We depict the indexing used in the proof of Proposition
7
, with
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
. As in Figure

, the subscripts increase modulo

\sigma_{m}
and
\sigma_{1}

\gamma_{2}

Proof.

The first result can be found in
[
23
,
7
]
, so we assume
s(\gamma_{2})
is notched throughout.

Case i)
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
do not lie in loops.

First, we assume we do not have that both
s(\gamma_{1})
is notched and the first crossing between
\gamma_{1}
and
T
is in this chain of spokes which
\gamma_{1}
crosses as it crosses
\gamma_{2}
, nor do we have the parallel configuration at
t(\gamma_{1})
. This means that if the elements
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
in Table
4
exist, they are elements of
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}
.

Any element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
outside the chain of spokes and loop will correspond to a unique element in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
and also in
\mathcal{P}_{5}\cup\mathcal{P}_{6}
. Therefore, we will focus on how
\Phi
acts on the elements in the chain of spokes and loop in each
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, and its action on the remaining elements will always be the natural one from this correspondence.

Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
, let
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
be the largest value in
I_{1}
in the chain of spokes, if it exists. Similarly, let
\sigma_{x}
be the largest value in
I_{2}
in the chain of spokes. By abuse of notation, we will also use
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
to denote the maximal elements on the chain of spokes in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and similarly for
\sigma_{x}
in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
.

We can write any
I_{1}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})
as
\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup I_{1}^{\prime}
, where even if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{\ell}^{(w)}
, we include in
I_{1}^{\prime}
everything not in the chain of spokes. We similarly can write
I_{2}=\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup I_{2}^{\prime}
. The elements in
I_{1}^{\prime}
and
I_{2}^{\prime}
have clear images in the other posets, so we will ignore them when we define the bijection.

We interpret any statement “
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(0)}
” as always true. If
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
or
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
do not exist, then we ignore any statement regarding these.

Subcase)
r>1
.

1.

Let
A_{1}
denote the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r+1)}
; we do not have both
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
and
y=k-1
; and, if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r)}
, then
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
, we set

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r+z-1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)&y<k-1\\
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r+z)}\rangle,\emptyset)&y=k-1\\
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r+z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)&y>k-1\\
\end{cases}

where if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist, we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}:=\sigma_{k-1}^{(z)}
. The set
\Phi(A_{1})
is all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
\sigma_{k-1}\notin I_{4}
.

2.

A_{2}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
;
y=k-1
; and,
z\leq w-r+1
where if
z=w-r+1
, then
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
, we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{k-1}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r+z-1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{k-1}\rangle)
where again
\sigma_{x}^{(r+z-1)}:=\sigma_{m}^{(r+z-2)}
if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist and we also set
\langle\sigma_{m}^{(r)}\rangle:=\emptyset
in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. The set
\Phi(A_{2})
is all
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{k-1}\in I_{4}
.

3.

B_{1}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r+1)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
and
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{1}
, we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z-(w-r))}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
if
\sigma_{x}
exists and otherwise set the image to
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z-(w-r))}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle)
. Notice that if
\ell\leq k-1
, the image of
(I_{1},I_{2})
has the same content as
(I_{1}\backslash(\langle\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r)}\rangle\cup\{\sigma_{1}^{(w-r+1)},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}^{(w-r+1)}\}))\cup I_{2}
and similarly if
\ell>k-1
, the image has the same content as
(I_{1}\backslash(\langle\sigma_{\ell}^{(w-r)}\rangle\cup\{\sigma_{1}^{(w-r+1)},\ldots,\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r+1)}))\cup I_{2}
. The set
\Phi(B_{1})
consists of all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}^{(r)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{6}
and
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{m}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{5}
.

4.

B_{2}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}^{(w-1)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{\ell}
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{2}
, we set the image to be
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z-(w-r))}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
. Notice that the image of
(I_{1},I_{2})
has the same content as
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash\langle\sigma_{\ell}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(B_{2})
consists of all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{\ell}^{(r)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{6}
and
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{m}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{5}
.

5.

B_{3}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{\ell}^{(w-1)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
and
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{\ell}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{3}
, if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succ\sigma_{\ell}^{(w-1)}
, we set the image to be
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)
and otherwise we set the image to be
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(r)}\rangle,\emptyset)
. The content of the image of
(I_{1},I_{2})
in this case is again the same as
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-r)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash\langle\sigma_{\ell}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(B_{3})
consists of pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{\ell}^{(r)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{6}
.

Now, let
r=1
. If
k-1\leq\ell
, then the same maps as above will work, so let
\ell<k-1
. We will partition the complement of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
in
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
now by
B_{2}^{0}\cup B_{3}^{0}
, which are defined as follows. Set
B_{2}^{0}
to be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-1)}\preceq\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
where the second inequality is equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)\in I_{2}
, and
\sigma_{\ell}\preceq\sigma_{x}\prec\sigma_{k-1}
where the first inequality is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. Set
B_{3}^{0}
to be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(w-1)}
and
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{k}
. We set
\Phi|_{B_{2}^{0}}(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(0)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
and
\Phi|_{B_{3}^{0}}(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(0)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)
. The image of
B_{2}^{0}
is pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\sigma_{k-1}\notin I_{6}
and the image of
B_{3}^{0}
is the complement. From this, one can construct a reverse map and observe that
\Phi:B_{2}^{0}\cup B_{3}^{0}\to J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
is a bijection.

Let
\mathbf{g}_{i}:=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{i}}
. We compare the quantities
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}
,
\mathbf{g}_{3}+\mathbf{g}_{4}
, and
\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}
. Recall that for a statement
X
,
\delta_{X}=1
if
X
is true and otherwise
\delta_{X}=0
, and if a statement involves an inequality, we require both objects in the comparison to exist for the statement to be true. We have the following
\mathbf{g}
-vectors, where
\mathbf{g}_{i}^{\prime}
denotes the contribution for the portion of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
which is not pictured in Table
4
.

•

\mathbf{g}_{1}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{\ell}}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(b+1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(a-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}

•

\mathbf{g}_{2}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)>\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}

•

\mathbf{g}_{3}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{\ell}}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(b+1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)>\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}+\mathbf{g}_{3}^{\prime}
.

•

\mathbf{g}_{4}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(a-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\mathbf{g}_{4}^{\prime}
.

•

\mathbf{g}_{5}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(a-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)>\mathcal{P}_{2}(2)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)}+\mathbf{g}_{5}^{\prime}

•

\mathbf{g}_{6}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{\ell+1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)>\mathcal{P}_{1}(b+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\mathbf{g}_{6}^{\prime}

From the definition of the posets
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, we can see that
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbf{g}_{3}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{4}^{\prime}=\mathbf{g}_{5}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{6}^{\prime}
. Thus, we immediately have
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{g}_{3}+\mathbf{g}_{4}
. We also can see that

(\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6})-(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2})=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{\ell}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{\ell+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(1)},

and by Corollary
1
, this vector is exactly
\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(r+1)}})=\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{p}^{w-r}\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots\hat{y}_{\sigma_{\ell}})=\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\hat{y}_{\sigma_{1}}\cdots\hat{y}_{\sigma_{\ell}})
.

Now suppose

. In

and

with the portion of a Hasse diagram shown below to the left.

To construct the bijections between the products of order ideals, we separately deal with sets

5.3
Type 2

Now, we consider an intersection between two arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
that, up to orientation, occurs before both arcs’ first intersection with
T
.

Let
p=s(\gamma_{2})
and

. Let the edge between
p
and
q
be labeled
\tau
. Following previous sections, let the remaining spokes incident to
p
be labeled
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m}
, indexed in counterclockwise order such that
\sigma_{1}
follows counterclockwise from
\tau
and
\tau
follows counterclockwise from
\sigma_{m}
. Similarly, label the remaining spokes incident to

with
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{h}

\tau
and
\tau
follows counterclockwise from
\eta_{h}
. In this configuration, the triangle where

and

and
\eta_{h}
. The arc
\gamma_{1}
must cross some consecutive sequence
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k}
of spokes incident to
p
and
\gamma_{2}
must cross a sequence
\eta_{\ell},\ldots,\eta_{h}
. It is possible for either sequence to consist of a single spoke, implying

or

, or for the arcs to wind several times around each puncture. Let
a
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{1}}(a)
denotes the arc (if it exists) that
\gamma_{1}
crosses immediately after this sequence of spokes, if it exists, and similarly let
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{2}}(b)
denote such an arc for
\gamma_{2}
.
