Proposition 8
.

Given
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
as described above, let
\gamma_{3}=\tau\circ\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{4}=\tau\circ\gamma_{2}
, and
\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{2}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{1}
, where
\gamma_{5}
is spliced at the intersection point of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
in their mutual last triangle. Then,

x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\tau}+Y_{\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}

where
Y_{\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(i)\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(i)}
.

Now we turn to our first new result.

Proposition 9
.

Given
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
which intersect in a Type 2 intersection, we have

x_{\gamma_{1}}x_{\gamma_{2}^{(p)}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\tau^{(p)}}+Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}Y_{\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}^{\geq 1}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}^{(p)}}

where

Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}^{\geq 1}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{2}(i)\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}\\
i\geq 1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(i)}.

Proof.

We follow our usual approach, partitioning
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
as
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup B
and constructing bijections between
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p)}})
and between
B
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
. We set
\mathcal{P}_{4}:=\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p)}}
. Recall the poset for
\tau^{(p)}
is the chain
\sigma_{1}\prec\sigma_{2}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{m}
.

In the following, let
\tau^{(v+1)}
be the element
\tau
in the loop of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. We repeat our notation from previous proofs, setting
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
to be the maximal element of a given order ideal of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on the chain of spokes and
\sigma_{x}
to be the maximal element on the loop of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. We let
\eta_{c}^{(d)}
similarly denote the maximal element of an order ideal of
\mathcal{P}_{2}
on the chain of spokes. We also use this notation for the other posets in the resolution.

1.

Let
A_{1}\subseteq J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(v+1)}\notin I_{2}
and if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
, with equality only if
\alpha\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
, we set

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)&y\neq 0\\
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle,\emptyset)&y=0\end{cases}

where in each case we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}:=\sigma_{0}^{(z)}
if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist. The set
\Phi(A_{1})
is the set of all
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
and if
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{h}^{(v)}
, then
y\neq 0
.

2.

A_{2}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(v+1)}\in I_{2}
and
z<w
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
, we define

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{(v+1)}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{0}^{(z+1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}^{(v)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)&y\neq 0\\
(\langle\sigma_{0}^{(z+1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}^{(v)}\rangle,\emptyset)&y=0\end{cases}

with the same convention when
\sigma_{x}
does not exist. The set
\Phi(A_{2})
is the set of all
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{h}^{(v)}
and
y=0
.

3.

A_{3}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(v+1)}\notin I_{2}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
which is strict if
\eta_{h}^{(v)}\in I_{2}
; and,
x\geq k
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. The map
\Phi
is defined on the spokes by
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(A_{3})
is the set of
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
which is strict if
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{h}^{(v)}
and
x\geq k
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
.

4.

B
consists of sets with
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w-1)}
, which is only strict if
\tau^{(v+1)}\in I_{2}
;
x\geq k
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{h}^{(v)}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, the set
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{0}^{(w-1)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash(\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}^{(v)}\rangle))
can be naturally partitioned into order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
and this map is bijective.

Finally, we must consider the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors. Let
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}
denote contribution of the parts of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[>a]
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}[>b]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2}
respectively. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+2\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}-2\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell}}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(b+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell}}+(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(b+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}+\mathbf{g}_{2}^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell-1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(a+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{2}(b+1)}-1)\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)}.

Since the poset for
\tau^{(p)}
comes with decoration
\tau^{-}
, we have the
\mathbf{g}
vector associated to it is
-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}
, so we have
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{e}_{\tau^{(p)}}+\mathbf{g}_{3}
.

Observe that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}\right)-\left(\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}\right)=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}-2\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}+2\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell-1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)}

which exactly matches the
x
-degree of
Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}Y_{\preceq\eta_{h}^{(v)}}^{\geq 1}
, as can be verified by using Corollary
1
two times.
∎

Recall that the content of an order ideal
I
of some poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
, denoted
\text{cont}(I)
, is the multiset of elements from
T
where
\tau\in T
shows up with multiplicity equal to the number of distinct elements of
I
correspond to
\tau
. In the following proof, concerning
\gamma_{1}^{(q)}
and
\gamma_{2}
, we can avoid the usual strategy by using the skein relation of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}^{(p)}
and noting that the two situations are related, up to relabeling, by reversing the posets. This also will explain why the
y
-monomial appears on a different term than in the rest of the section. We will employ a similar tactic in the proof of Proposition
14
.

Proposition 10
.

Given
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
which intersect in a Type 2 intersection, we have

x_{\gamma_{1}^{(q)}}x_{\gamma_{2}}=y_{\tau}x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\tau^{(q)}}+x_{\gamma_{5}^{(q)}}x_{\gamma_{6}}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma_{4}:=\tau^{(q)}
and set
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
, using the versions from the statement of the proposition. For each
i
, let
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
be the dual poset to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. That is, this is a poset on the same elements where each relation is reversed. One can observe that, up to different labeling conventions, this set of posets resembles those used in Proposition
9
.
Therefore, from the proof of that proposition, there exists a partition
\overline{A}\cup\overline{B}=J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}})\cup J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}})
and bijections between
\overline{A}
and
J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}})\cup J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{4}})
and between
\overline{B}
and
J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{5}})\cup J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{6}})
.

In any poset, the complement of an order ideal is an order filter, so the above bijections could instead be phrased in terms of order filters. Now, the order filters of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{i}}
are exactly the order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, so we recover a bijection
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})\to(J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4}))\cup(J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6}))

Now we check that this bijection has the desired properties to give the skein relation.

Given any order ideal
\overline{I}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{i}
, let
I_{i}
be its complement viewed as an order ideal in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. Let
A=\{(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2}):(\overline{I_{1}},\overline{I_{2}})\in\overline{A}\}
and similarly for
B
.
Given
(\overline{I_{1}},\overline{I_{2}})\in\overline{A}
with
\overline{\Phi}(\overline{I_{1}},\overline{I_{2}})=(\overline{I_{3}},\overline{I_{4}})
, we have
\text{cont}(\overline{I_{1}}\cup\overline{I_{2}})=\text{cont}(\overline{I_{3}}\cup\overline{I_{4}})
. Therefore,
\text{cont}(\overline{I_{1}}\cup\overline{I_{2}})
is bounded above by
\text{cont}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}_{4}})=\text{cont}(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\cup\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}})\backslash\{\tau\}
. Equivalently,
\{\tau\}\subseteq\text{cont}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
for all
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A
. The fact that
\overline{\Phi}:\overline{A}\to J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{3}})\times J(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{4}})
preserved content will mean that there is a bijection
\Phi:A\to J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that for all
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A
, if
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})=(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
, then
\text{cont}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})\backslash\{\tau\}=\text{cont}(I_{3}\cup I_{4})
.

Let
S
be the multiset
\{\sigma_{1}^{w},\sigma_{2}^{w},\ldots,\sigma_{k}^{w},\sigma_{k+1}^{w-1},\ldots,\eta_{1}^{v-1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell-1}^{v-1},\eta_{\ell}^{v},\ldots,\eta_{h}^{v},\tau^{w+v-2}\}
and notice this multiset corresponds to the indices of the
Y
-monomial in the skein relation in Proposition
9
. There are some special cases when
\sigma_{k}=\tau
or
\eta_{\ell}=\tau
but these are handled similarly. Now, given
(\overline{I_{1}},\overline{I_{2}})\in\overline{B}
, we know that
S\subseteq\text{cont}(\overline{I_{1}}\cup\overline{I_{2}})
, so
\text{cont}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
is bounded above by
\text{cont}(\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2})\backslash S=\{\tau,\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{h}\}
. Here we are using a relabeling, since in the case where
\gamma_{2}
is notched, the set
S
contains both chains of spokes but not the loop
\sigma_{1}\prec\cdots\prec\sigma_{m}\prec\tau
, and then we relabel these as
\eta_{i}
. Since
(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}})\in\overline{B}
, we know
(\emptyset,\emptyset)\in B
. We can conclude that
\Phi
is a weight-preserving bijection between
B
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
.

Now, we have shown the desired bijection (notice the sets
A
and
B
swapped roles with the duality). The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computations would also follow a dual version of those in Proposition
9
, so we omit this.
∎

We turn to the final and most complicated scenario of this section, which is where
s(\gamma_{1})
and
s(\gamma_{2})
are both notched.

Proposition 11
.

Given
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
as previously described, let
\gamma_{3}=\tau\circ\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{4}=\tau\circ\gamma_{2}
, and
\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{2}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{1}
, where
\gamma_{5}
is spliced at the intersection point of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
in their mutual last triangle. Then,

\displaystyle x_{\gamma_{1}^{(q)}}x_{\gamma_{2}^{(p)}}

\displaystyle=x_{\tau^{(p,q)}}x_{\gamma_{5}}+Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}x_{\gamma_{5}^{(q)}}x_{\gamma_{6}^{(p)}}+

where

Y_{\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(1)}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}.

Proof.

In this case,
\mathcal{P}_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p,q)}}
, which is shown below using the notation from this section.

\tau^{-}
\sigma_{1}
\vdots
\sigma_{m}
\eta_{1}
\vdots
\eta_{h}
\tau^{+}

We set
\sigma_{0}=\eta_{0}=\tau
. We will sometimes denote the minimal element
\tau
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as
\tau^{(1)}
or
\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
and similarly the maximal element
\tau
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
as
\tau^{(v+1)}
or
\eta_{0}^{(v+1)}
.

The partitions we use will depend on whether
w=1
or
w>1
and similarly for
v
. We begin with the most general case, following the usual proof strategy.

Case i)
w>1,v>1
.

We partition
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
and define maps from each part to
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
or
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
as follows.

1.

A_{1}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that if
\eta_{h}\in I_{1}
, then
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{1}
or
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
; if
y=m
, then
c\neq 0
; if
c=h,y\neq 0
;
\tau^{(v+1)}\notin I_{2}
;
d\geq 2
; and,
z\leq w-1
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
,
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}\rangle,\langle\eta_{f}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)
, so that
\Phi(A_{1})
consists of all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
satisfying if
c=h
, then
\sigma_{1}\in I_{4}
or
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{3}
; if
I_{4}=\emptyset
, then
z=1
; if
d=v
and
f=h
, then
y\neq 0
;
\tau^{+}\notin I_{4}
;
d\geq 2
; and,
z\leq w-1
.

2.

A_{2}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
satisfying
y=m
;
c=0
; and,
z\leq w-1
with equality only if
x\leq k
and this latter is only equality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
,
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{m}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\eta_{0}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}^{(d)}\rangle)
so that
\Phi(A_{2})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
I_{4}=\emptyset
and
z\geq 2
.

3.

A_{3}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
satisfying
d=1
;
z\leq w-1
; if
f=h
, then
y\neq 0
; and, if
y=m
, then
f\neq 0
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{3}
,
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\eta_{c}^{(1)}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
so that
\Phi(A_{3})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
satisfying
d=1
;
z\leq w-1
; and,
I_{4}=\emptyset
.

4.

A_{4}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
satisfying
d\geq 2
;
z=w
; if
x=w
, then
c\neq 0
; and, if
c=h
, then
x\neq 0
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{4}
,
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}^{(d)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}\rangle)
so that
\Phi(A_{4})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
satisfying
d\geq 2
;
z=w
; and,
I_{4}\neq 0
.

5.

A_{5}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
and
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\preceq\eta_{0}^{(2)}
where (1) if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
and
d=1
, then
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{k}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
, (2) if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
and
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{0}^{(2)}
, then
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{k}
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
, and (3) if
z=w
and
d=1
, then
x=m
only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{2}
,
f=h
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{1}
, and
y=0
and
c=h
only if
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{k}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
, and (4) we do not allow
d=2
and
z=w
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{5}
, we have

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(1)}\rangle,\emptyset)&z=w-1\text{ and }d=1\\
(\langle\sigma_{0}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)&z=w-1\text{ and }d=2\\
(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)&z=w\text{ and }d=1.\\
\end{cases}

We have
\Phi(A_{5})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
satisfying
d=1
;
z=w
; and,
\tau^{+}\notin I_{4}
.

6.

A_{6}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
satisfying
y=0
; if
d=1
, then
f=h
; and, if
d>1
, then
c=h
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{6}
, we have

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{0}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\eta_{c}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z-1)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)&d=1\\
(\langle\eta_{f}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z-1)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)&d>1.\\
\end{cases}

We have
\Phi(A_{6})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\tau^{+}\in I_{4}
;
z<w
; and, if
d=v
, then
y\neq 0
.

7.

A_{7}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
x=m
;
z=w
;
c=0
; and, if
d=2
, then
f\geq\ell-1
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)\in I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{7}
, we have
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\eta_{0}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{f}^{(d-1)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)
so
\Phi(A_{7})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
satisfying
\tau^{+}\in I_{4}
;
z=w
; and,
d<v
.

8.

A_{8}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(v+1)}\in I_{2}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{8}
, we have

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\tau^{(v+1)}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\eta_{f}^{(v)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)&y=0\text{ or }z=w\\
(\langle\eta_{f}^{(v)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{0}^{(z+1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)&y\neq 0\text{ and }z<w.\end{cases}

We have
\Phi(A_{8})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that (
\tau^{+}\in I_{4}
;
d=v
; and,
y=0
), (
\tau^{+}\in I_{4}
;
d=v
; and,
z=w
), or (
d=v
;
f=h
; and,
y=0
).

9.

A_{9}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
c=h
and
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\notin I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{9}
,
\Phi(\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\eta_{h}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{h}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
so that
\Phi(A_{9})
consists of
(I_{3},I_{4})
with
c=h
;
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\notin I_{3}
; and,
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{4}
.

10.

B
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
x\geq k
which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
;
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\preceq\eta_{0}^{(2)}
which is equality only if
\sigma_{0}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
;
f\leq\ell-1
which is equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{2}(b)\in I_{2}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
which is only strict if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
or
\eta_{h}^{(1)}\in I_{2}
;
\sigma_{1}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
;
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
only if
\eta_{h}^{(1)}\in I_{2}
; and,
\{\sigma_{1}^{(w)},\eta_{1}\}\subset I_{1}
only if
\eta_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{2}
. There is a straightforward bijection between this set and
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
where the content of the image is the same as the content of
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle)
.

Case ii)
w=1,v>1
.

We now describe how to adjust the sets and maps in the general case to this case. Let
A_{1}
be the analogue of
A_{4}
and
A_{2}
be the analogue of
A_{7}
from the general case, and define
\Phi
in a similar way on these. Here,
\Phi(A_{1})
consists of all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
\tau^{+}\notin I_{4}
; if
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\in I_{3}
then
\tau^{-}\in I_{4}
; and, if
c=h
, then
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\in I_{3}
, and
\Phi(A_{2})
consists of all
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\tau^{+}\in I_{4}
. The set
B
in this case is obtained by interpreting the set from the general case in an appropriate way for
w=1
. The remaining parts of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
can be divided in a simpler way than in the general case.

3.

A_{3}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(1)}\notin I_{1}
;
\sigma_{x}
exists; and,
x\leq k
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. Given such a pair, we set
\Phi(\emptyset,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle,\emptyset)
so that the image of
A_{3}
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
with
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\in I_{3}
and
I_{4}=\emptyset
.

4.

A_{4}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{(1)}\notin I_{1}
;
x\geq k
which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
d>1
. Given such a pair, we set
\Phi(\emptyset,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{h}^{(d-1)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
so that the image of
A_{4}
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
c=h
;
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\notin I_{3}
; and,
x\geq k
which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
.

5.

A_{5}
consists of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{x}
does not exist and
c=h
. Given such a pair, we set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{h}^{(d)}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{h}^{(d)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
so that the image of
A_{5}
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
c=h
;
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}\notin I_{3}
; and,
x\leq k
which is only equal if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
.

All other cases)

Adjusting to
w>1
and
v=1
can be done similarly to adjusting from Case i to Case ii. When
w=1
and
v=1
, one can use a simpler partitioning, which we omit here. One can see a similar strategy used in the proof of Proposition
14
where the full details are provided.

The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computations are straightforward and need not be broken into these cases, so we omit them.
∎

5.4
Intersection with arcs in the triangulation

In this section, we deal with intersections between
\gamma\notin T
and
\tau
such that
\tau^{0}\in T
. We can recognize these intersections by the presence of
\tau^{0}
in the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}
; if
\tau^{0}
appears in the loop portion of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
, then this is an incompatibility which was dealt with in Section
4
.

The resolution of
\gamma
and
\tau
will result in two multicurves, each containing two curves. If we ignore possible notchings, then we can describe the posets of these four curves as the following.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[<i]\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{>i}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>i]\backslash\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
.
Define
\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i}
similarly, using “
\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
” instead in the superscripts. If
\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
is the multicurve with a
Y
-coefficient in the expansion of
x_{\gamma}x_{\tau}
, we will either have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{>i},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i}
or the roles of lower and upper will be swapped. This will depend on the notching on
\tau
and the local configuration of
\gamma
near its intersection point with
\tau
.

Throughout this section, let
i
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)=\tau^{0}
. We define a set
F
which will determine most of the
y
-monomial in the relations presented here. Begin with
F=\emptyset
. For both possible signs, we decide whether to add certain sets to
F
. There are several cases of
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
based on the possible notchings.

•

If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\notin\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, include the order ideal generated by
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)
to
F
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, include the order filter generated by
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i\pm 1)
to
F
.

The following Lemma is used implicitly throughout this section.

Lemma 4
.

For any pair
(\gamma,\tau)
such that
e(\gamma,\tau)>0
,
F
is either empty, an order ideal, or an order filter.

Proof.

Assume
F\neq\emptyset
. Up to reindexing the chronological ordering,
F
would fail to be an order ideal or order filter if we have
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i-1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i-1)\notin\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
. Clearly, the three arcs
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i-1),\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i),\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)
must share a vertex, call it
p
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, it must be that
\tau
is notched at
p
. However, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i-1)\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
as well, a contradiction.
∎

First, let
\tau=\tau^{0}
so that
\tau
is a plain arc. This type of intersection was also studied by Pilaud, Reading, and Schroll in
[
10
]
. Here
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}=\emptyset
and
F\cup\{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\}=\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle
. In this case, we have that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{>i},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i}
. Throughout, we set
Y_{F}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\in F}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)}
.

Proposition 12
.

Let
\tau\in T
and let
\gamma
be an arc such that
e(\gamma,\tau)>0
. Pick
i
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)=\tau
. Let
Y_{\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\in\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)}
. Then,

x_{\gamma}x_{\tau}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{F}y_{\tau}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}.

Proof.

We describe a bijection
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma})\to(J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}))\cup(J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}))
as follows. Let
A
be the subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma})
given by all order ideals which do not contain
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
and let
B
be the subset of all order ideals containing
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
. We see that an element of
A
restricts to order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
while if
I\in B
,
I\backslash\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle
restricts to an order ideal of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}
. Combining these maps gives the desired bijection, with a clear inverse map.

There are several cases to consider when comparing the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors. We provide one case in-depth; the others are similar or simpler, and many follow from skein relations in the unpunctured case. Suppose that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)
so that in particular
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
is neither minimal nor maximal. Suppose moreover that
s(\gamma)
is notched, so that there is a loop
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(0)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(-1)\prec\cdots\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(-m)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(1)
.

Recall the
\mathbf{g}
-vector for
\tau\in T
is
\mathbf{e}_{\tau}
. We verify that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\mathbf{g}_{\tau}
. We have a summand
\mathbf{e}_{\tau}
in the vector
\mathbf{r}_{\gamma_{3}}
. The maximal element closest to
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)
, does not appear in either
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
or
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
, but we have a summand
\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)}
in
\mathbf{r}_{\gamma_{4}}
. All other terms appear for the same reasons in
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}
and
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
.

Now we compare
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\mathbf{g}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{5}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{6}}
. The arc
\gamma_{5}
as defined will be
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(0)^{(s(\gamma))}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{5}}=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(-1)}
where
\alpha
is the third arc in the triangle formed by
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(0)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(-1)
. Let
\beta
be the third arc in the triangle formed by
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)
. Then,
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{6}}=\mathbf{e}_{\beta}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)}+\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{g^{\prime}}
consists of all contributions to
\mathbf{g}
from values
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>i]
. There are implicitly two cases:
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+2)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+2)
.

From Lemma
2
, we know that

\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{\langle\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\rangle})=\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\hat{y}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)})=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{e}_{\beta}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma(-1)}}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma(i+1)}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(0)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma(i)}},

and we can conclude that this agrees with
(\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{5}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{6}})-(\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\mathbf{g}_{\tau})
.
∎

Now we consider an intersection with
\gamma
and
\tau=\tau^{(p)}
singly-notched. Let
\alpha,\beta,\sigma_{1},
and
\sigma_{m}
be the arcs in the quadrilateral around
\tau
in
T
, written in clockwise order such that
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{m}
are incident to the notched endpoint
p
. The elements in
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
will be
\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}\}
.

\beta
\alpha
\sigma_{m}
\sigma_{1}
\tau
\bowtie
p

In order to describe the posets that arise in the various cases here, we introduce a term. Suppose there is a puncture
p
with spokes
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
, and let
\gamma
be an arc such that the first arc crossed by
\gamma
forms a triangle with two consecutive spokes
\sigma_{k}
and
\sigma_{k+1}
. Then, we say that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
has
a loop at the beginning based at
p
. We could similarly say a poset has
a loop at the end based at
p
. Trivially, a poset that only consists of a loop both begins and ends at a loop.

Orient
\tau
from its notched endpoint to its plain endpoint. We know that, up to a choice of orientation, either
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
immediately before crossing
\tau
,
\gamma
crosses
\beta
immediately before crossing
\tau
, or
\tau
is the first arc which
\gamma
crosses, so that
i=1
. If we are in the first case or
\tau
is the first arc which
\gamma
crosses and
s(\gamma)
is plain, we set
\gamma_{3}=\gamma\circ\tau
,
\gamma_{4}=\tau\circ\gamma
,
\gamma_{5}=\gamma\circ\tau^{-1}
and
\gamma_{6}=\tau^{-1}\circ\gamma
where the resolution is
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
. The corresponding posets are as follows.

•

\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}^{0}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i})^{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
has a loop at the beginning based at
p
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{0}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i})^{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
has a loop at the end based at
p
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{>i}
.

It is possible for
(\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i})^{0}
or
(\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i})^{0}
to be empty, in which case
\gamma_{4}
or
\gamma_{5}
would be notched versions of arcs from
T
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
or
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
will be decorated posets.

If
\gamma
crosses
\beta
immediately before crossing
\tau
, or
\tau
is the first arc which
\gamma
crosses and
s(\gamma)
is notched, then we flip the definition of
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{5}
and flip
\gamma_{4}
and
\gamma_{6}
. This is to reflect which term in the expansion is multiplied by a monomial in
y
-variables.

In this case,
F
will either be an order ideal or an order filter based on which arcs near
\tau

\gamma
crosses. The
y
-monomial will also possibly have an extra factor, depending on whether
\gamma_{5}^{0}
or
\gamma_{6}^{0}
is in
T
and is notched at
p
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
\tau\in T
and let
\gamma
be an arc such that
e(\gamma,\tau)>0
. Pick
i
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)=\tau
. Set
Y_{F}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\in F}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)}
. If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{0}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i})^{0}
and this set is empty, set
\delta_{5}=1
. Similarly if
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}^{0}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{2})^{0}
and this set is empty, set
\delta_{6}=1
. If
\delta_{i}
is not yet specified, set
\delta_{i}=0
. Then,

x_{\gamma}x_{\tau^{(p)}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{F}y_{\gamma_{5}^{0}}^{\delta_{5}}y_{\gamma_{6}^{0}}^{\delta_{6}}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}

Proof.

Throughout let
\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma},\mathcal{P}_{2}:=\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p)}}
, and for
3\leq i\leq 6
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
.

Case i)
\gamma
crosses
\beta
immediately before crossing
\tau
.

In this case, there will never be
j
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}
, so
F
will be
F=\langle\beta\rangle
.

Subcase a)
\gamma
crosses
\beta
;
\tau
is the last arc crossed by
\gamma
; and,
t(\gamma)
is plain.

Here
\gamma_{6}=\sigma_{m}^{(p)}
. In this case,
\mathcal{P}_{6}^{0}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i})^{0}=\emptyset
, so that
\delta_{6}=1
. We partition
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup B
where
A_{1}
is the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau\notin I_{1}
and
\beta\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
,
A_{2}
is the set of pairs such that
\tau\in I_{1}
and
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
, and
B
is the set of pairs such that
\beta\in I_{1}
,
\sigma_{m}\notin I_{2}
, and
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\tau\in I_{1}
.

We see that
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
is in bijection with
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})
, where we send each element from
\mathcal{P}_{1}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}
to its image in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
(note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{4}=(\emptyset,\alpha)
here), and
B
is in bijection with
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
where
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\beta\rangle)\cup I_{2}
restricts to order ideals of each.

For the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors, suppose
n=|\mathcal{P}_{1}^{0}|
, so that
\beta=\mathcal{P}_{1}(n-1)
. Set
\mathbf{g}_{i}
to correspond to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
. We have
\mathbf{g}_{1}=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n-1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(d-2)}\mathbf{e}_{\beta}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{g}_{2}=-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\beta}
,
\mathbf{g}_{3}=\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(n-1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(d-2)}\mathbf{e}_{\beta}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{g}_{4}=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}
where
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime}
records the contributions of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[<n-1]
to
\mathbf{g}_{1}
. We immediately see
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}=\mathbf{g}_{3}+\mathbf{g}_{4}
.

Let
x
be the largest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x+1)
is minimal in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. If
x+1>1
, let
\mu
be the third arc in the triangle formed by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x+1)
. Then we have
\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{F})=\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(x+1)}-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)}+\mathbf{e}_{\mu}-\mathbf{e}_{\beta}-\mathbf{e}_{\tau}+\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}
. If
x+1=1
, replace
\mu
(
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)
) with the counterclockwise (clockwise) neighbor of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x+1)
in the first triangle
\gamma
passes through. We have
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{5}}=-\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)}+\mathbf{e}_{\mu}+\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
where
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
now refers to the contribution of
\mathcal{P}_{1}[\leq x]
to
\mathbf{g}
, and
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{6}}=\mathbf{g}_{\sigma_{m}^{(p)}}=\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{e}_{\tau}
. If we rewrite
\mathbf{g}_{1}
where we only set aside
\mathbf{g}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, we can see that
\mathbf{g}_{1}+\mathbf{g}_{2}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{F})=\mathbf{g}_{5}+\mathbf{g}_{6}
.

Subcase b)
\gamma
crosses
\beta
;
\tau
is the last arc crossed by
\gamma
; and,
t(\gamma)
is notched.

Notice here we have
\gamma_{4}=\alpha^{(t(\gamma))}
and
\gamma_{6}=\sigma_{m}^{(p,t(\gamma))}
. We already know
\sigma_{m}
and
\alpha
are spokes at
t(\gamma)
. Let the remaining spokes be labeled
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{h}
. In particular, the loop at the end of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is
\tau\succ\sigma_{m}\prec\eta_{1}\prec\cdots\prec\eta_{h}\prec\alpha\succ\tau
where
\tau\in(\mathcal{P}_{1})^{0}
, and the poset
\mathcal{P}_{4}
is the chain
\sigma_{m}\prec\eta_{1}\prec\cdots\prec\eta_{h}
.

1.

We define
A_{1}
in the same way as the previous case, and given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
, we map these to a pair
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
where elements from the loop in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
are sent to the loop in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
elements from the loop in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
are sent to the loop in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. The image of
A_{1}
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
\tau\notin I_{3}
.

2.

Let
A_{2}
denote the subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\tau\in I_{1}
;
\alpha\notin I_{1}
;
\sigma_{m-1}\in I_{2}
; and,
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
. Pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
can be naturally mapped to pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
\tau\in I_{3}
. This completes our bijection between
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
.

3.

Let
B
be the complement of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
in
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
and notice that
B
can be characterized as all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\langle\beta\rangle\subseteq I_{1}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\tau\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{m-1}\in I_{2}
only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
only if
\alpha\in I_{1}
. There is a natural bijection between
B
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
which completes our overall bijection.
The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computation is similar enough to the plain case that we suppress it.

Subcase c)
\gamma
crosses
\beta
immediately before and
\alpha
immediately after crossing
\tau
.

We will assume
\delta_{6}=0
; if this is not the case, one can combine the following bijections with the ideas from this first case. We also automatically know
\delta_{5}=0
because
\mathcal{P}_{5}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
.

To be consistent with our description of this case, we choose a chronological ordering such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i+1)=\alpha
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i-1)=\beta
. Therefore, the set
F
in this case is
\langle\mathcal{P}_{1}(i-1)\rangle=\langle\beta\rangle
. Even though
\gamma
could cross
\beta
and
\alpha
multiple times, below we write
\beta
and
\alpha
to signify the entries
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i\pm 1)
.

1.

A_{1}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\beta\notin I_{1}
.

2.

A_{2}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\beta\in I_{1}
;
\tau\notin I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
.

3.

A_{3}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau\in I_{1}
;
\alpha\notin I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
.

4.

B
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\beta\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\tau\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
only if
\alpha\in I_{1}
.

Then, we can map any tuple
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
to a tuple
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})
, where the map in each case is clear since each element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
has a clear image in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
or
\mathcal{P}_{4}
. Similarly, for any
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, there is a natural way to partition
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\beta\rangle)\cup I_{2}
into order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
In each case, these operations are clearly invertible.
The
\mathbf{g}
-vector calculations follow similarly from the previous case.

Subcase d)
\gamma
crosses
\beta
immediately before and
\sigma_{m}
immediately after crossing
\tau
.

We again specify the chronological ordering such that
\beta=\mathcal{P}_{1}(i-1)
and
\sigma_{m}=\mathcal{P}_{1}(i+1)
. Let
k\in[m]
be such that the last spoke that
\gamma
crosses as it winds around
p
immediately after crossing
\tau
is
\sigma_{k}
. If this spoke is
\tau
, set
k=0
.

If
k\neq 0
, let
i^{\prime}\geq i
be the largest value such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i^{\prime})=\tau
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i^{\prime})\preceq\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)
; it is possible that
i^{\prime}=i
. If
k=0
, let
i^{\prime}
be the second largest value. By our assumption that
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{m}
immediately after crossing
\tau=\sigma_{0}
, this value will exist.

1.

A_{1}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\beta\notin I_{1}
.

2.

A_{2}
is the set of tuples such that
\beta\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
; and,
\tau=\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
.

3.

B
is the set of tuples such that
\beta\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i^{\prime})\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{k}\notin I_{2}
; and, if
i^{\prime}>i
,
\mathcal{P}_{1}(i^{\prime}-1)\notin I_{1}
.

The bijections and the
\mathbf{g}
-vector calculations are similar to the first case, so we again omit them. We stress that again, given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, we partition
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\beta\rangle)\cup I_{2}
into order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
.

Case ii)
\mathbf{\gamma}
crosses
\mathbf{\sigma_{1}}
immediately before crossing
\tau
.

In this case,
F
will be the order filter generated by
\sigma_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{1}(i-1)
.

Some parts of this case resemble Type 1 grafting (Section
5.2
), so we will streamline some sections, including
\mathbf{g}
-vectors.

We index the chain
\mathcal{P}_{1}[<i]^{\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}
as in previous sections (see for example Figure
9
):
\sigma_{k}^{(w)}\succ\sigma_{k-1}^{(w)}\succ\cdots\succ\sigma_{1}^{(w)}\succ\tau^{(w)}\succ\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\succ\cdots\succ\sigma_{1}^{(1)}
for some
w\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq m
. Again, we set
\sigma_{0}:=\tau
, and if
k=0
, then the maximal element would be
\sigma_{0}^{(w+1)}
with our convention for changing superscripts. We extend the indexing and set
\mathcal{P}(i)=\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
. As in previous sections, we let
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
be the largest element from the chain
\mathcal{P}_{1}[<i]^{\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)}
in
I_{1}
, if it exists, and let
\sigma_{x}
be the largest element in
I_{2}
, if it exists. We also let these symbols refer to the largest elements in chain of spokes and loop in the posets from the resolution. Finally, we let
a<i
be the largest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\not\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(i)
, if it exists.

Subcase a)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
immediately before and
\alpha
immediately after crossing
\tau
.

We partition
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
and describe appropriate maps. In the following, we assume
\delta_{5}=\delta_{6}=0
; this is equivalent to assuming
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
exists.

1.

A_{1}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that we do not have both
y=m
and
\alpha\in I_{1}
and if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
, then
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{k}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{1}
, define

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle)&y\neq m\\
(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z+1)}\rangle,\emptyset)&y=m,\end{cases}

where if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist, we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}:=\sigma_{0}^{(z)}
.
For any other element of
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
, there is a clear corresponding element in
\mathcal{P}_{3}\cup\mathcal{P}_{4}
so we define
\Phi
to map between these. The set
\Phi(A_{1})
is the collection of all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
x<m
and if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
.

2.

A_{2}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
y=m
;
\alpha\in I_{1}
; and,
z\leq w-1
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{2}
, we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{m}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(A_{2})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
and

\sigma_{m}\in I_{4}
.

3.

A_{3}
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
z=w
;
x\geq k
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\notin I_{1}
; and,
x=m
only if
\alpha\in I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in A_{3}
, we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(A_{3})
is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
z=w
, and
x\geq k
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\notin I_{3}
, which is the complement of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
in
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
.

4.

B
is the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
, which is only strict if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
;
x\geq k
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
\alpha\notin I_{1}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B
, there is a clear way to partition
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle)
into order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
and this map is bijective.

Notice that as sets
\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle
has the same content as
F
, which in this case is the principal order filter generated by
\sigma_{1}^{(1)}
.

Subcase b)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
;
\tau
is the last arc crossed by
\gamma
; and,
t(\gamma)
is plain.

This case follows from the previous (Case ii.a) by ignoring
A_{2}
and any conditions involving
\alpha
.

Subcase c)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
;
\tau
is the last arc crossed by
\gamma
; and,
t(\gamma)
is notched.

In this case,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
has a loop at its end consisting of the chain
\sigma_{m}\prec\eta_{1}\prec\cdots\prec\eta_{h}\prec\alpha
, attached to
\tau
, and
\gamma_{4}=\sigma_{m}^{(p,t(\gamma))}
. As in other places, we let
\sigma_{m}^{-}
and
\sigma_{m}^{+}
denote the minimum and maximum respectively of
\mathcal{P}_{4}
.

1.

We use the same set
A_{1}
as in Case ii.a with the added condition that if
y=m-1
, then
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
. This allows us to use the same map. Now,
\Phi(A_{1})
consists of all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{m}^{+}\notin I_{4}
; if
z=w
, then
x\leq k
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{3}
; and, if
z>1
, then
I_{4}\neq\emptyset
.

2.

We can nearly use the same set
A_{2}
, but we include a special case where
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
is the minimal element
\sigma_{m}
in the loop in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. In this case, we send
(\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
to
(\emptyset,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
. The image
\Phi(A_{2})
consists of all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{m}^{+}\in I_{4}
and
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
.

3.

We modify
A_{3}
to include the stipulation that if
\sigma_{x}\preceq\sigma_{m-1}
, then
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
. The map and image of this set are the same as in Case ii.a.

4.

We define
A_{4}
to be the set of tuples
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
satisfying
y=m-1
, and if
z=w-1
, then
x\leq k
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. We set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{m-1}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z+1)}\rangle,\emptyset)
. The image of
A_{4}
is the set of
(I_{3},I_{4})
where
I_{4}=\emptyset
and
z>1
.

The complement of
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{4}
will be called
B
and this can be characterized by
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\alpha\notin I_{1}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m-1}^{(w-1)}
, which is strict if
\eta_{1}\in I_{1}
;
z=w
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
; and,
x\geq k
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. As before, there is a way to partition
(I_{1}\backslash\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle)\cup(I_{2}\backslash\langle\sigma_{k}\rangle)
into order ideals of
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
and this map is bijective.

Subcase d)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
immediately before and
\alpha
immediately after crossing
\tau
.

This entire situation is nearly identical to the calculations for Type 1 resolutions in Section
5.2
. One can for example see the similarity in the definition of the
y
-monomial in the relation.

Case iii)
\tau
is the first arc crossed by
\gamma
.

The labeling of the resolution in this case depends on the notching of
s(\gamma)
. In particular,
F=\emptyset
regardless of the notching, but it is possible
\delta_{5}=1
or
\delta_{6}=1
. The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computations are all straight forward in this case since the
y
-monomial is degree 1, so we will not address them.

Subcase a)
\tau
is the first arc crossed by
\gamma
;
\alpha
is the second arc; and,
s(\tau)
is plain.

In this case as well as the next,
\delta_{6}=1
, so the
y
-monomial will be
y_{\gamma_{6}^{0}}=y_{\sigma_{1}}
.
This case is solved by setting
A=(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\tau\in I_{1}
and
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
only if
\alpha\in I_{2}
. Then, the complement is characterized by
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
;
\tau\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
; and,
\alpha\notin I_{1}
.

Subcase b)
\tau
is the first arc crossed by
\gamma
;
\sigma_{m}
is the second arc; and,
s(\tau)
is plain.

We set
A
to be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{1}
and
\sigma_{x}\leq\sigma_{k-1}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{2}
. The map from
A
to
J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
is clear; note that
\mathcal{P}_{3}=(\emptyset,\beta^{+})
.

We will divide the complement of
A
into three smaller sets. Note that in order to have
(I_{1},I_{2})\notin A
, it must be
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
.

1.

Let
B_{1}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
. We define
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)
, so that the image consists of
(I_{5},I_{6})
with
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
and
\sigma_{x}\prec\tau
.

2.

Let
B_{2}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
where
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
and
\sigma_{x}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}
, which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. For such a pair, we define
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{0}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\tau\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(1)}\rangle)
so that the image consists of
(I_{5},I_{6})
with
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(1)}
and
\sigma_{x}=\tau
.

3.

Let
B_{3}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
where
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(2)}
. If
y\neq 1
, we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle)
and if
y=1
we set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{1}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\emptyset,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle)
. The image consists of all
(I_{5},I_{6})
with
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{1}^{(2)}
, which completes our bijection.

Subcase c)
\tau
is the first arc crossed by
\gamma
;
\alpha
is the second arc; and,
s(\tau)
is notched.

In this case and in the next case,
\delta_{5}=1
, so the
y
-monomial will be

. Here, we set
A=(I_{1},I_{2})
such that

only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
;
\tau\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
; and,
\alpha\notin I_{1}
. The complement,
B
, can be characterized as pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\beta\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\tau\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{m}\in I_{2}
only if
\alpha\in I_{1}
. In each case, we see that there is a clear bijection between the subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
and the appropriate product of lattices from the resolution.

Subcase d)
\tau
is the first arc crossed by
\gamma
;
\sigma_{m}
is the second arc; and,
s(\tau)
is notched.

We let
A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}
, as described below.

1.

Let
A_{1}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
f=h
;
y=1
;

; and, if

, then
x\geq k-1
which is strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
. We set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{1}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{1}^{+}\rangle,\sigma_{x}^{(z)})
where if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}=\sigma_{0}^{(z)}
. The image of

is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{1}^{+}\in I_{3}
.

2.

Let
A_{2}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(2)}
where
y\neq 1
if
f=h
and
y\neq 0
if
\beta\in I_{1}
. We define

\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=\begin{cases}(\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle&y\neq 0\\
(\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(z)}\rangle)&y=0\\
\end{cases}

where as in other cases we set
\sigma_{x}^{(z)}=\sigma_{0}^{(z)}
if
\sigma_{x}
does not exist. The image of

is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that
\sigma_{1}^{+}\notin I_{3}
and
z>1
.

3.

Let
A_{3}
consist of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
z=1
;if
\beta\in I_{1}
, then
\sigma_{x}
exists; and, if
\eta_{h}\in I_{1}
, then
x\geq 2
. We set
\Phi(\langle\sigma_{y}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle)=(\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle)
. The image of

is all pairs
(I_{3},I_{4})
such that

and

.

The complement of

can be characterized as pairs

such that

;

only if

or
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{2}\in I_{2}
only if
\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{k-1}\in I_{2}
only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{1}^{(2)}
only if
\eta_{h}\in I_{1}
;
\sigma_{2}^{(2)}\notin I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{k}\notin I_{2}
. The map in this case is fairly clear, but since there are two elements associated to
\sigma_{1}
, a little care is needed to be consistent. We set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{h}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{1}^{(2)}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\sigma_{1}\rangle,\langle\tau\rangle)
and
\Phi(\langle\eta_{h}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{0}^{(2)}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\eta_{h}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{1}\rangle)
.

∎

Now, we turn to an intersection between
\gamma\notin T
and
\tau^{(p,q)}
such that
\tau^{0}\in T
. We now have that the endpoints of
\tau
are punctures
p
and
q
; these could possibly be equal but it suffices to consider the case where they are distinct. As before, we will have the spokes at
p
being
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m}
, and now let the spokes at
q
be
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{h}
, as below. In this case,
\{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j):\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\text{ and }\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(j)\notin\mathcal{P}_{\tau}\cup\{\tau^{0}\}\}
is empty since all elements comparable to
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
will also correspond to elements in
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}\cup\{\tau^{0}\}
, so
F
is an order filter.

Since the set
F\cup\{\tau\}
is always an order filter, there is no ambiguity in labeling the posets in the resolution such as there was in the case for
\tau^{(p)}
. We set
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}^{0}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{<i}
with a loop at the end based at
q
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}^{0}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{upper}}^{>i}
with a loop at the beginning based at
p
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}^{0}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{<i}
with a loop at the end based at
p
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}^{0}=\mathcal{P}_{\text{lower}}^{>i}
with a loop at the beginning based at
p
.

Proposition 14
.

Let
\tau\in T
and let
\gamma
be an arc such that
e(\gamma,\tau)>0
. Pick
i
such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)=\tau
. If
\gamma_{5}^{0}\in T
, let
\delta_{5}=1
and similarly for
\gamma_{6}^{0}
and
\delta_{6}
. If
\delta_{i}
is not yet specified, set
\delta_{i}=0
. Then, ,

x_{\gamma}x_{\tau^{(p,q)}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{F}y_{\tau}y_{\gamma_{5}^{0}}^{\delta_{5}}y_{\gamma_{6}^{0}}^{\delta_{6}}x_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}

Proof.

Throughout let
\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma},\mathcal{P}_{2}:=\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p)}}
, and for
3\leq i\leq 6
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
.

Case i)
\gamma
crosses
\eta_{h}
and
\sigma_{m}
immediately before and after crossing
\tau
.

Throughout this case, we will have
F=\emptyset
and
\delta_{5}=\delta_{6}=0
, so the
y
-monomial in the skein relation will always be
y_{\tau}
. We draw diagrams for
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{3}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{5}
below, using similar labeling to many previous proofs. In particular,
\sigma_{0}:=\tau
and
\eta_{0}:=\tau
, and the superscripts change between
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{m}
,
\sigma_{0}^{(i)}\succ\sigma_{m}^{(i-1)}
and similarly in the chain of
\eta_{c}^{(d)}
.

\mathcal{P}_{1}

\mathcal{P}_{3}

\mathcal{P}_{5}

\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
\sigma_{k}^{(1)}
\ddots
\sigma_{m}^{(w)}
\tau^{top}
\eta_{h}^{(v)}
\iddots
\eta_{\ell}^{(1)}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)

\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
\eta_{\ell-1}
\eta_{\ell}
\vdots

\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
\eta_{\ell}^{(1)}
\iddots
\eta_{h}^{(v)}
\tau^{top}
\sigma_{1}
\vdots

The poset
\mathcal{P}_{2}
is exactly
\mathcal{P}_{\tau^{(p,q)}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{4}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
are similar to
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and
\mathcal{P}_{5}
respectively, with the roles of
\sigma
’s and
\eta
’s reversed. Notice that we have
\tau^{top}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{5},\mathcal{P}_{6}
. In
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\tau^{top}
can also be called
\eta_{0}^{(v+1)}
or
\sigma_{0}^{(w+1)}
, and we have similar options for labeling in
\mathcal{P}_{5}
and
\mathcal{P}_{6}
.

Note that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)
need not exist. If it is the case that one of these does not exist, then throughout we ignore any condition involving them.

As in previous proofs, let a generic element
I_{1}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})
with
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
be
\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle
where we as always ignore elements outside of the loop and chain of spokes since it is clear how
\Phi
should act on them. If there is no element in
I_{1}
from the chain from
q
, we write
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{\ell-1}^{(1)}
and similarly for the chain from
p
we write
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{k-1}^{(1)}
. We let a generic element
I_{2}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
be
\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
, where if
I_{2}\neq\emptyset
, we set
\eta_{v}=\eta_{0}
if
\eta_{1}\notin I_{2}
and similarly for
\sigma
. By abuse of notation, we also let the support of an arbitrary set
I_{5}
on the chain of spokes and loop be denoted
\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle
and similarly for
I_{6}
,
\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle
. Recall
F=\{\tau\}
, so each map
\Phi:B_{i}\to J(\mathcal{P}_{5})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{6})
will be such that
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{i}
has the same content as
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})\cup\{\tau\}
.

The proof becomes simpler when we this case into more cases based on how much
\gamma
winds around each puncture.

Subcase)
v>1
and
w>1
.

1.

A
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
I_{2}=\emptyset
;
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\preceq\eta_{\ell-1}^{(2)}
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. There is a clear bijection between
A
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
.

2.

B_{1}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{2}=\emptyset
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
; and,
(\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
, strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
) or
(\eta_{c}^{(d)}\succeq\eta_{\ell-1}^{(2)}
, strict if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
). If it is the case that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
, then we set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z-1)}\rangle)
. If the above is not true, then it must be that
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\succeq\eta_{\ell-1}^{(2)}
with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
, and we set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d-1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(B_{1})
consists of pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5},I_{6}
, and either (
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
;
\eta_{1}\notin I_{6}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
) or (
\eta_{h}\in I_{6}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{5}
;
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\prec\eta_{0}^{(v)}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{6}
).

3.

B_{2}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{2}\neq\emptyset
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
;
\tau^{+}\notin I_{2}
;
\eta_{h}^{(v)}\in I_{1}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
; and,
\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{2}
. If
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{2}
, then we set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(B_{2})
consists of all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5}
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{6}
;
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{6}
; and,
\eta_{h}\in I_{6}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{5}
.

4.

B_{3}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{2}\neq\emptyset
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
;
\tau^{+}\notin I_{2}
; and,
(\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
and
\eta_{1}\notin I_{2})
or

and
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{2}
). If

and

, then we set

. Otherwise, if that is not true, then it must be that
\eta_{h}^{(v)}\in I_{1}
and

, and we set

. The set
\Phi(B_{3})
consists

such that

and either (

;

; and,

) or (

;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{5}
; and,
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\succeq\eta_{0}^{(v)}
, which is strict if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{m}^{(w)}
).

5.

B_{4}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{+}\in I_{2}
;
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\prec\eta_{0}^{(v)}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
. Given
(I_{1},I_{2})\in B_{4}
, we set

. The set
\Phi(B_{4})

;

;

;

with strict inequality if

; and,

.

6.

B_{5}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that

;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
; and

. Otherwise, we have

, only equal if
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}=\sigma_{m}^{(w)}
, and
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
and here we set
\Phi(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(w)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)=(\langle\eta_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}\rangle\langle\tau^{top}\rangle)
. The set
\Phi(B_{5})
consists of pairs

such that (

and

) or (
