and
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\preceq\eta_{0}^{(v)}
which is only equal if
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
).

7.

B_{6}
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{1}
. For the special cases
I_{2}=\emptyset
and
I_{2}=\langle\tau^{+}\rangle
, we set
\Phi(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\eta_{0}^{(v)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)
and
\Phi(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\tau^{+}\rangle)=(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\tau^{top}\rangle)
. For any other nonempty
I_{2}=\langle\eta_{f}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{z}\rangle
, we have
\Phi(I_{1},I_{2})=(\langle\eta_{f}^{(v)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,)
. The image
\Phi(B_{6})
contains
(\langle\eta_{0}^{(v)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)
and pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{6}
and
\eta_{c}^{(d)}\succeq\eta_{0}^{(v)}
with strict inequality if
x=m
.

Subcase)
w>1
and
v=1
.

Now, we consider the case where the arc
\gamma
only winds multiple times around one of the punctures, which is without loss of generality chosen to be
p
. We explain how we can modify the partition and maps from the previous case.

1.

A
is now the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{2})
such that
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
;
\eta_{f}\preceq\eta_{\ell-1}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
\tau^{-}\in I_{2}
only if
\eta_{h}^{(1)}\in I_{1}
. The bijection between
A
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})
is obvious.

2.

To define
B_{1}
, we only use the condition involving
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}
. The image, using the same map, is the set of
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5}
;
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
;
\eta_{1}\notin I_{6}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\prec\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
.

3.

B_{2}
and
\Phi|_{B_{2}}
are defined the same way as the general case, and so the image is also described identically.

4.

We set
B_{3}
to be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
I_{2}\neq\emptyset
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
;
\tau^{+}\notin I_{2}
; and one of the following is true:

(a)

\sigma_{m}^{(w)}\in I_{1}
and
\eta_{1}\notin I_{2}
;

(b)

\eta_{c}^{(1)}=\eta_{h}^{(1)}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{2}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
;

(c)

\eta_{c}^{(1)}=\eta_{h}^{(1)}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{2}
; and,
\eta_{f}\succeq\eta_{\ell-1}
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
.

If a set can be described by property (a), then we use a map as in the general case, and the image of this subset is the set of
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5}
;
\eta_{1}\notin I_{6}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
.
If a set cannot be described by (a) but can be described by (b), then we set the image to be
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z-1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
, so that the image is the set of
(I_{5},I_{6})
where
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
with equality only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{6}
; and,
\eta_{f}\prec\eta_{h}
. If a set cannot be described by (a) or (b) but can be described by (c), then we set the image to be
(\langle\eta_{f}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\eta_{h}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle)
, so that the image consists of
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5}
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{6}
;
\eta_{h}\in I_{6}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{5}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{k-1}^{(2)}
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{6}
.

5.

To define
B_{4}
, we replace the condition involving
\eta_{c}^{(d)}
with
\eta_{c}^{(1)}\prec\eta_{h}^{(1)}
. We define the map the same way, so that the image can be described also in the same way except with no condition on
\eta_{c}^{(d)}
.

6.

B_{5}
is defined by now using the conditions (
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
and
\eta_{c}^{(d)}=\eta_{h}^{(1)}
) or (
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
which is strict if
\eta_{c}^{(1)}
). If we are in the first case, we set the image to be
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{c}^{(d)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)
, so that the image of this portion can be described as all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
;
\eta_{f}=\eta_{h}
; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\preceq\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
. If we are in the second case, then we use the same map as in the general case, and the image of this subset is all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{6}
and
\tau^{top}\notin I_{5}
.

7.

B_{6}
is defined the same way as the general case. For the special case where
I_{2}=\emptyset
, we define
\Phi(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\emptyset)=(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}\rangle)
and for all other cases we use the same map as the general case. Thus, the set
\Phi(B_{6})
consists of pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
;
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succeq\sigma_{m}^{(w-1)}
with equality only if
\eta_{f}
does not exist; and,
\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\succ\sigma_{0}^{(w)}
if
\eta_{f}=\eta_{h}
.

Subcase)
w=v=1
.

Since this subcase is much more specialized, not every set from the general case will have a clear analogue here.

1.

A
is the set of
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{-}\in I_{2}
only if
\eta_{h}^{(1)}
or
\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
;
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\in I_{1}
;
\eta_{1}\in I_{2}
only if
\eta_{h}^{(1)}\in I_{1}
;
\{\sigma_{1},\eta_{1}\}\subset I_{2}
only if
\tau^{top}\in I_{1}
;
f\leq\ell-1
with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
; and,
x\leq k-1
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
. There is a small ambiguity here between the images of
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\emptyset)
and
(\langle\eta_{h}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{m}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\tau^{-}\rangle)
; we arbitrarily set the former to be (
\langle\eta_{h}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{0}\rangle)
and the latter to be
(\langle\eta_{0}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{m}\rangle)
.

2.

Here, we set
B_{2}
to be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{-}\in I_{2}
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
;
\tau^{+}\notin I_{2}
;
\eta_{h}^{(1)}\in I_{2}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{2}
; and,
\eta_{h}^{(1)}
and
\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
are not in
I_{1}
. We use a map as before, so that the image is all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\eta_{h}\in I_{6}
only if
\sigma_{1}\in I_{5}
and
\sigma_{m}\in I_{5}
only if
\eta_{1}\in I_{6}
.

3.

We let
B_{3}
be all pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that on the following is true:

(a)

\eta_{c}^{(1)}=\eta_{h}^{(1)}
;
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{2}
; and,
f\geq\ell-1
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1}
;

(b)

\sigma_{y}^{(1)}=\sigma_{m}^{(1)}
;
\eta_{1}\notin I_{2}
; and,
x\geq k-1
, with strict inequality
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
.

If (a) describes a pair
(I_{1},I_{2})
, then we set the image to be
(\langle\eta_{f}^{(1)}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{y}^{(z)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{h}\rangle)
, so that the image is all pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\eta_{h}\in I_{6}
;
\tau^{top}\notin I_{6}
; and,
\sigma_{1}\notin I_{5}
. The image is defined dually for a pair described by (b); notice that no pair can be described by both (a) and (b).

4.

Let
B_{5}
be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{+}\in I_{2}
and
\tau^{top}\notin I_{1}
. If
\eta_{\ell}^{(1)}\in I_{1}
, we set the image of such a pair to be
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\eta_{c}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(1)}\rangle)
and otherwise we set the image to be
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\eta_{\ell-1}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{y}^{(1)}\rangle)
so that the image is the set of pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
and
f\geq\ell-1
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{5}
.

5.

Let
B_{6}
be the set of pairs
(I_{1},I_{2})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
and (
f\geq\ell-1
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{1})
or (
x\geq k-1
, with strict inequality if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\in I_{1}
). If the first condition is true, then we set the image to be
(\langle\eta_{f}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\sigma_{x}\rangle,\langle\tau^{top}\rangle)
and if only the second condition is true then we set the image to be
(\langle\tau^{top}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{x}^{(1)}\rangle\cup\langle\eta_{f}\rangle)
. Thus, the image is pairs
(I_{5},I_{6})
such that
\tau^{top}\in I_{6}
or
\tau^{top}\in I_{5}
and
f\leq\ell-1
, with equality only if
\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\in I_{5}
.

Now, we consider the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors, which by Corollary
1
will not depend on the winding numbers. We have
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}=\mathbf{e}_{\tau}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{k}}-\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{\ell}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(a-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(a)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(b+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(b)}+\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
is the contribution of elements in
\mathcal{P}_{1}[<a]\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}[>b]
to
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}
. Then, noting
\mathbf{g}_{\tau^{(p,q)}}=-\mathbf{e}_{\tau}
, we can quickly see
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\mathbf{g}_{\tau^{(p,q)}}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
.
Similarly,
Y_{F}=y_{\tau}
and
\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{y}_{\tau})=\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{m}}+\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{h}}-\mathbf{e}_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{\eta_{1}}
, and we can see
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\mathbf{g}_{\tau^{(p,q)}}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{y}_{\tau})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{5}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{6}}
.

Cases ii and iii)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
immediately before crossing
\tau
and
\tau
is not the last arc crossed by
\gamma
.

There are two cases here, based on which arc
\gamma
crosses after crossing
\tau
, and each strongly resembles Type 1 resolutions (Section
5.2
).

Case iv)
\gamma
crosses
\sigma_{1}
and
\eta_{1}
immediately before and after crossing
\tau
.

This can be derived from Case i in the same way that we could prove Proposition
10
from Proposition
9
. That is, the posets involved here are duals of the posets for the first case, and one can check that the dual version of the bijections follow the desired format to show the skein relation, as is detailed in Section
3.4
.

Case v)
\tau
is the first or last arc crossed by
\gamma
.

There are a variety of subcases within this case. However, each can be derived from a more general case in the same manner as we dealt with such a configuration in the proof of Proposition
12
.
∎

6
Self-Crossings

We consider here skein relations of the form
x_{\gamma}=x_{C^{+}}+Yx_{C^{-}}
which correspond to to an arc which is incompatible with itself. Proposition
3
dealt with arcs
\gamma
with incompatible tagging, so here we only need to consider arcs with a self-intersection. An arc can have a self-intersection of Type 0, 1, or 2.

If
\gamma
has a self-intersection of Type 1, let
p
be the starting or ending point of
\gamma
near this point of intersection, and suppose
\gamma
winds counterclockwise around
p
at this point of intersection. Then, Proposition
7
holds when we label the resolution
\{\gamma_{34}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
and replace
x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}
with
x_{\gamma_{34}}
.

If
\gamma
has a self-intersection of Type 2, then let
\gamma_{3}
be the closed curve. Then, the results from Section
5.3
hold when we label the resolution
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
and replace
\gamma_{5}\gamma_{6}
with
\gamma_{56}
. The untagged version of these two cases come from Theorem 7.4 in
[
24
]
.

There are multiple ways in which an arc can have a self-intersection of Type 0. Recall this will mean that the poset will have a pair of isomorphic subposets, such that one lies at the bottom and one lies at the top of the whole poset. Because these are now subposets of the same poset, there are added complications that can arise. One such scenario is when, given a chronological ordering of the poset
\mathcal{P}
and a pair of isomorphic subposets
\{\mathcal{P}(s),\ldots,\mathcal{P}(t)\}
and
\{\mathcal{P}(s^{\prime}),\ldots,\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime})\}
, the isomorphism between these posets reverses them, sending
\mathcal{P}(s+i)
to
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}-i)
.We call this a “reverse overlap”. It is also possible for the subposets to “kiss” or intersect, so that for some
i
,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)
is in one subposet and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i+1)
is in the other. It is not geometrically possible for a reverse overlap to be kissing or intersecting as well, so these complications will thankfully not compound.

In Section
6.1
, we deal with overlaps which do not kiss or intersect, including reverse overlaps. In these cases, we can use methods similar to those for two distinct intersecting arcs. In Section
6.2
we address a self-intersection which results in a kissing overlap. We do not address overlaps which intersect as taggings on arcs do not affect these. That is, even in a punctured surface, Theorem 4.12 from
[
24
]
holds as stated.

In general, the resolution of a self-intersection results in one set containing an arc and a closed curve and another set containing a single arc. We will refer to these as
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
or
\{\gamma_{34}\}\cup\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}
depending on which scenario we are in and which term gets a
y
-monomial in the expansion.

6.1
Non-intersecting and non-kissing overlaps

Suppose
\gamma
has a self-intersection resulting in
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
, isomorphic subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
which have a crossing overlap. Here, we suppose that
R_{1}\cap R_{2}=\emptyset
and that
t\neq s^{\prime}-1
nor
t^{\prime}=s-1
. We have two cases, based on whether, with a chronological ordering which has
s<t
, we have
s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}
(
a forward overlap
) or
t^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
(
a reverse overlap
).

Suppose first that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
has a forward overlap, so that with the same chronological ordering we have
s<t
and
s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}
. Then, we set
C^{+}=\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}
where
\gamma_{4}
is a closed curve and
C^{-}=\{\gamma_{56}\}
. Constructing the corresponding posets is similar to the two arc case, detailed in Section
5.1
. For instance, to describe
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
, one takes
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[\leq t^{\prime}]\cap\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>t]
, so that one has
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t+1,t^{\prime}]
with added relation
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t+1)
, resulting in a circular fence poset. One can similarly combine the descriptions of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}
in Section
5.1
to describe
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
here.

Proposition 15
.

Let
\gamma
be an arc with a self-intersection which appears as a forward, non-intersecting, and non-kissing crossing overlap in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
. Let
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
be the resolution of this intersection. Then,
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Yx_{\gamma_{56}}
where
Y
is defined as follows ,

Y=\begin{cases}Y_{R}&(s>1\textbf{ or }s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is notched})\textbf{ and }(t<n\textbf{ or }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is plain})\\
Y_{R}Y^{\leq(s^{\prime}-1)}_{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}&s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is plain}\textbf{ and }(t<n\textbf{ or }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is plain})\\
Y_{R}Y^{\geq(t+1)}_{\succeq(t^{\prime}+1)}&(s>1\textbf{ or }s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is notched})\textbf{ and }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is notched}\\
Y_{R}Y^{\leq(s^{\prime}-1)}_{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}Y^{\geq(t+1)}_{\succeq(t^{\prime}+1)}&s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is plain}\textbf{ and }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is notched}\\
\end{cases}

where

Y_{R}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\in R_{1}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}\qquad Y^{\leq(s^{\prime}-1)}_{\preceq(s^{\prime}-1)}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime}-1)\\
i\leq s^{\prime}-1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}\quad\text{and}\quad Y^{\geq(t+1)}_{\succeq(t^{\prime}+1)}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+1)\\
i\geq t+1\end{subarray}}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}.

The proof of these relations follows exactly the format of the proof of Proposition
6
.

Now, we turn to reverse overlaps. While the type of arcs which appear will be the same, we will see that the
y
-monomial will now appear with the product of an arc and closed curve.

We again let
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
be isomorphic posets with chronological ordering and indexing chosen so that
s<t
,
R_{1}
is on top, and
R_{2}
is on bottom. We also assume
s<s^{\prime}-1
; if we have the opposite case, the construction would be similar. Since this is a reverse overlap, we have
t^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
. When the overlap is in reverse, one can check that it is impossible to have
R_{1}\cap R_{2}
be nonempty. Abstractly, we could have the case
t^{\prime}=t+1
, but since we consider triangulations without self-folded triangles, we would never have an arc cross the same arc in the triangulation two times consecutively. Therefore, we assume that these overlaps do not kiss; that is,
t+1<t^{\prime}
.

We define
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{34}}
to be
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
with the chronological ordering on
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t+1,t^{\prime}-1]
reversed. That is, when listing the elements using the chronological ordering, this has the form
\cdots\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}-1)\cdots\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t+1)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime})\cdots
. Notice this poset has the same content as
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
, but the sets
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
no longer form a crossing overlap.

The poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{5}}
will be the circular fence poset on
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t+1,t^{\prime}-1]
where, since we know
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t),\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t+1)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}-1)
form a triangle, we know there is a relation
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t+1)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}-1)
. The poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}
can be defined similarly to how the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{6}}
was defined in Section
5.1
.

Proposition 16
.

Let
\gamma
be an arc with a self-intersection which appears as a reverse crossing overlap in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
. Let
\{\gamma_{34}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
be the resolution of this intersection. Then,
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{34}}+Yx_{\gamma_{5}}x_{\gamma_{6}}
where
Y
is defined as follows, using analogous definitions to Proposition
15
,

Y=\begin{cases}Y_{R}&(s>1\textbf{ or }s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is notched})\textbf{ and }(t<n\textbf{ or }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is plain})\\
Y_{R}Y^{\geq(s^{\prime}+1)}_{\preceq(s^{\prime}+1)}&s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is plain}\textbf{ and }(t<n\textbf{ or }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is plain})\\
Y_{R}Y^{\leq(s-1)}_{\succeq(s-1)}&(s>1\textbf{ or }s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is notched})\textbf{ and }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is notched}\\
Y_{R}Y^{\geq(s^{\prime}+1)}_{\preceq(s^{\prime}+1)}Y^{\leq(s-1)}_{\succeq(s-1)}&s=1\textbf{ and }s(\gamma)\text{ is plain}\textbf{ and }t=n\textbf{ and }t(\gamma)\text{ is notched.}\\
\end{cases}

The proof of Proposition
16
also directly follows the ideas in the proofs of Proposition
6
.

It is also possible for
\gamma
to have a self-intersection which resembles the Type 1 and Type 2 crossings discussed in Sections
5.2
and
5.3
respectively.

If
\gamma
has a self-intersection of Type 1, let
p
be the starting or ending point of
\gamma
near this point of intersection, and suppose
\gamma
winds counterclockwise around
p
at this point of intersection. Then, Proposition
7
holds when we label the resolution
\{\gamma_{34}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
and replace
x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}
with
x_{\gamma_{34}}
.

If
\gamma
has a self-intersection of Type 2, then let
\gamma_{3}
be the closed curve. Then, the results from Section
5.3
hold when we label the resolution
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
and replace
\gamma_{5}\gamma_{6}
with
\gamma_{56}
. The untagged version of these two cases come from Theorem 7.4 in
[
24
]
.

6.2
Kissing Overlaps

In this section, we consider an arc
\gamma
with self-intersection such that its poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
has a kissing overlap. Precisely,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
has isomorphic subposets
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
such that
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
,
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
, and
t+1=s^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, we suppose
R_{1}
is on top and
R_{2}
is on bottom, so that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
. Let
\alpha
denote the third arc in the triangle formed by
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s^{\prime})
. We necessarily have that
s>1
and
t^{\prime}<n=|\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}|
; otherwise, this intersection would be covered by the Type 1 or Type 2 intersections, and the proof would follow immediately from the two arc case. Moreover, notice that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s-1)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+1)
both correspond to
\alpha
.

Let
b\geq 1
be the largest value such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s-b)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b)
correspond to the same arc in
T
; call this arc
\beta
. Set
\beta^{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s-b),\beta^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b),\alpha^{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s-1)
and
\alpha^{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+1)
. If
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s-b-1)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b+1)
exist, call these
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
respectively. We emphasize that
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
are distinct arcs while
\alpha^{1}
and
\alpha^{2}
stand for two copies of the same arc in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
and similarly for
\beta^{1},\beta^{2}
. Moreover, there are two options for the configuration of
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
, depending on
\gamma
. We draw below a diagram of the relevant arcs from
T
, as well as the two ways that
\gamma
can achieve this kissing overlap.

\beta
\alpha
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t)
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(s)
\epsilon_{i}
\epsilon_{j}

Set
M_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s-b,s-1]
and
M_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t^{\prime}+1,t^{\prime}+b]
. The two choices for the orientation of
\gamma
correspond to whether
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting or intersecting overlaps, as below.

\epsilon_{1}
\boxed{M_{1}}

\epsilon_{2}

Since either end of
\gamma
may be notched, let
n^{\prime}\leq 1
and
n^{\prime\prime}\geq d
be such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}]
.

We draw below the resolution in the case where
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting (i.e. the left picture above), with
s-b>1
, and
t^{\prime}+b<n
. The intersecting case is identical except for the fact that
\gamma_{56}
now has a contractible kink. Recall from Definition
5
that we define
x_{\gamma_{56}}
to be negative of the expression associated to
\gamma_{56}
with the kink removed.

Let the resolution of
\gamma
be denoted
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
where
\gamma_{3}
and
\gamma_{56}
are arcs and
\gamma_{4}
is a closed curve, as in the figures above. Then,
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
is the poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[\leq t]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>t^{\prime}]
, where we have the relation
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+1)
, which matches the orientation of the associated arcs. The poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
is the circular fence poset on
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
with added relation
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s)\succ\mathcal{P}_{1}(t)
.

The arc
\gamma_{56}
, with poset
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
can vary based on
s-b
and
t^{\prime}+n
as well as the tagging at each endpoint of
\gamma
.

1.

If
s-b>1
and
t^{\prime}+b<n
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is the poset on
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[<s-b]\cup\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>t^{\prime}+b]
where the relation between
\mathcal{P}_{1}(s-b)
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(t^{\prime}+b)
follows from the configuration of
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
.

2.

If
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b<n
, let
b<a\leq n-t^{\prime}
be the largest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t^{\prime}+b+1,t^{\prime}+a]
correspond to a set of arcs which are all incident to
s(\gamma)
. If
t^{\prime}+a=n
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b)\succ\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b+1)
(
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b+1)
), let
\zeta\in T
be the arc which is clockwise (counterclockwise) of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(d)
in the last triangle
\gamma
passes through.

(a)

If
t^{\prime}+a<n
, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[>t^{\prime}+a]
.

(b)

Suppose
t^{\prime}+a=n
and
s(\gamma)
is plain. If
t(\gamma)
is plain, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is the poset associated
\zeta
and if
t(\gamma)
is notched, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is the poset associated to
\zeta^{(t(\gamma))}
.

(c)

Suppose
t^{\prime}+a=n
and
s(\gamma)
is notched. If
t(\gamma)
is plain, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is the poset for
\zeta^{(s(\gamma))}
and if
t(\gamma)
is notched, then
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
is the poset for
\zeta^{(s(\gamma),t(\gamma))}
.

3.

If
s-b>1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
, we have a dual set of conditions to above to describe
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}}
.

4.

If
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
, then
\gamma_{56}
is contractible and we do not associate a poset to
\gamma_{56}
. If the taggings on
s(\gamma)
and
t(\gamma)
are the same, then
x_{\gamma_{56}}=0
. If the taggings are different, then
x_{\gamma_{56}}
is given by Definition
7
.

We will use the letters
a
and
b
as described here in the following Proposition and proof.

Proposition 17
.

Let
\gamma
be an arc such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
has a crossing overlap in regions
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
with
t+1=s^{\prime}
. Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be the overlaps which come before
R_{1}
and after
R_{2}
. Let
Y_{R}=\prod_{i=s}^{t}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
and
Y_{M}=\prod_{i=s-b}^{s-1}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
.
Then,
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}\pm Yx_{\gamma_{56}}
where the sign is
+
when
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting and is
-
when
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are intersecting, and the monomial
Y
is determined by the following conditions.

1.

If
s-b>1
and
t^{\prime}+b<n
,
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}
.

2.

If
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b<n
and

(a)

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting and
s(\gamma)
is plain, then
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}
.

(b)

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are intersecting and
s(\gamma)
is plain, then if
t(\gamma)
is plain,
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}\prod_{i=t^{\prime}+b+1}^{t^{\prime}+a}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
and if
t(\gamma)
is notched,
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}\prod_{i=t^{\prime}+b+1}^{t^{\prime}+a+1}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
.

(c)

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting and
s(\gamma)
is notched, then
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}\prod_{i=t^{\prime}+b+1}^{t^{\prime}+a}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(i)}
.

(d)

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are intersecting and
s(\gamma)
is notched, then
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}
.

3.

If
s-b>1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
,
Y
is defined in a parallel way to part 2.

4.

If
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
,
Y=Y_{R}Y_{M}
.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
and for
i=3,4,56
,
\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{i}}
. For shorthand, we will refer to the set
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s,t]
in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
as
R_{3}
, the set
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s-b,s-1]
as
M_{3}
, and the set
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[t^{\prime}+1,t^{\prime}+b]
in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
as
M_{4}
. The posets
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}}
for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
non-intersecting (left) and intersecting (right) are drawn below.

\epsilon_{1}
\boxed{M_{3}}
\boxed{R_{3}}
\boxed{M_{4}}
\epsilon_{2}

\epsilon_{1}
\boxed{M_{3}}
\boxed{R_{3}}
\boxed{M_{4}}
\epsilon_{2}

We will begin with part 1. Even when
\gamma
is notched at one or both endpoints, this case follows from translating the proof mechanics in Section 4.5 of
[
24
]
to our poset setting. We will still go into detail for this case as it will streamline our remaining cases, which are distinct from the work in
[
24
]
.

Case i)
s-b>1
;
t^{\prime}+b<n
; and,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting.

Let
A_{1}\subset J(\mathcal{P})
be the set of all
I_{1}
such that we do not have
R_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
and
(R_{2}\cup\{\alpha^{2}\})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
. Given
I_{1}\in A_{1}
,
\Phi
sends the elements from
R_{1}
to the corresponding elements in
R_{3}
and the elements from
R_{2}
to
\mathcal{P}_{4}
until the first switching position, if it exists, working from
\mathcal{P}(s)
and
\mathcal{P}(s^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{P}(t)
and
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime})
and then it swaps where these go. If this switching position does not exist, it must have been
R_{1}\cup\{\alpha^{2}\}\subseteq I_{1}
and
R_{2}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
. In this case, we send
R_{1}
to
R_{3}
. The elements of
M_{1}
go to the corresponding elements in
M_{3}
and similarly for
M_{2}
and
M_{4}
, and all other elements have clear images in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
. The image of this map is all tuples
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
except those of the form
(I_{3},I_{4})
with
(R_{3}\cup\{\alpha^{2}\})\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
and
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
. There is a clear inverse map from this subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
back to
A_{1}
, which sends elements in
R_{3}
to
R_{1}
and in
\mathcal{P}_{4}
to
R_{2}
until the first switching position (using the same ordering on these as we did for
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
).

Now, let
A_{2}\subset J(\mathcal{P})
be the set of all
I_{1}
such that that
R_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
;
(R_{2}\cup\{\alpha^{2}\})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
; and, if we have both
M_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
and
M_{2}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
, then
\epsilon_{2}\in I_{1}
and
\epsilon_{1}\notin I_{1}
. The map
\Phi|_{A_{2}}
acts as follows. If there is a switching position between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
,
\Phi|_{A_{2}}
sends the elements of
M_{1}
to
M_{3}
and the elements of
M_{2}
to
M_{4}
until the first switching position, working from
\mathcal{P}(s-b)
to
\mathcal{P}(s-1)
and from
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b)
to
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+1)
. If
M_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
and
M_{2}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
,
\Phi
send the elements of
M_{1}
to
M_{4}
. We set
\Phi(R_{1})=\mathcal{P}_{4}
, and the other elements have clear images. The image of this subset of
J(\mathcal{P})
is tuples of the form
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
where
R_{3}\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
and
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
. By instead considering the first switching position between
M_{3}
and
M_{4}
, we can form an inverse map from this subset of
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
to
A_{2}
.

Finally, let
B=J(\mathcal{P})\backslash(A_{1}\cup A_{2})
, which can be characterized as the set of
I_{1}
such that
R_{1}\cup M_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
;
(R_{2}\cup M_{2})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
; and,
\epsilon_{2}\in I_{1}
only if
\epsilon_{1}\in I_{1}
. Then,
\Phi(I_{1})
sends
I_{1}
to the order ideal
I_{1}\backslash(R_{1}\cup M_{1})
in
J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{56}})
. The inverse map is clear in this case.

Next, we check the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors. By comparing the shape of
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
with the expansion in Lemma
2
, we see that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}&=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ maximal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ minimal }\end{subarray}}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s-b,s-1)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ maximal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s-b,s-1)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ minimal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}\\
&+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s)\succ\mathcal{P}(s+1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s)\prec\mathcal{P}(s+1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(s)}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}(t-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t)\prec\mathcal{P}(t-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(t)}\\
&+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-1)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-2)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-1)\prec\mathcal{P}(s-2)})\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b)\prec\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\beta}\\
&+\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-b-2)}\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{1}}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+1)\succ\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+2)})\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{2}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime}\end{split}

(2)

where
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime}
reflects the contributions of elements in
\mathcal{P}[<s-b-1]\cup\mathcal{P}[>t^{\prime}+b+1]
.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ maximal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ minimal }\end{subarray}}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s-b,s-1)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ maximal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}-2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s-b,s-1)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ minimal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}

\displaystyle-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s)\prec\mathcal{P}(s+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(s)}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t)\succ\mathcal{P}(t-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(t)}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-1)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-2)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-1)\prec\mathcal{P}(s-2)})\mathbf{e}_{\alpha}+(\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b)\prec\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)})\mathbf{e}_{\beta}

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)\succ\mathcal{P}(s-b-2)}\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{1}}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+1)\succ\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+2)})\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{2}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime}

and

\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}=\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s)\succ\mathcal{P}(s+1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(s)}-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime})\prec\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}-1)}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ maximal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in(s,t)\\
\mathcal{P}(x)\text{ minimal }\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathcal{P}(x)}

so we have
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
.

From Lemma
2
, the first three lines of Equation
6.2
are
\deg_{x}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{M})
. Since
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{56}}=\delta_{\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)\prec\mathcal{P}(s-b-2)}\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{1}}-(1-\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+1)\succ\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+2)})\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{2}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime}
, we can see that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{M})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{56}}
.

Case ii)
s-b>1
;
t^{\prime}+b<n
; and,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are intersecting.

Since our relation is now of the form
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}-Yx_{\gamma_{56}}
, we construct a bijection
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P})\cup J(\mathcal{P}_{56})\to J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
\Phi
restricted to
\mathcal{P}
preserves content, and given
I_{56}\in\mathcal{P}_{56}
,
\text{cont}(I_{56})=\text{cont}(\Phi(I_{56}))\cup R_{1}\cup M_{1}
.

1.

Let
A_{1}\subseteq J(\mathcal{P})
and the definition of
\Phi|_{A_{1}}
be as in case i.

2.

Let
A_{2}\subseteq J(\mathcal{P})
be the set of
I_{1}\in J(\mathcal{P})
such that
R_{1}\subseteq I_{1}
;
(R_{2}\cup\{\alpha^{2}\})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
; and, such that we do not have both
M_{1}\subset I_{1}
and
M_{2}\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
. We describe
\Phi|_{A_{2}}
. The set
R_{1}
is sent to
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}
. If we have a switching position between
I_{1}|_{M_{1}}
and
I_{1}|_{M_{2}}
, then we map the elements of
M_{1}
to
M_{4}
and the elements of
M_{2}
to
M_{3}
until the first switching position where we switch, working from
\mathcal{P}(s-b)
to
\mathcal{P}(s-1)
and from
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b)
to
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+1)
. The only way we cannot have a switching position is when
(M_{1}\cup M_{2})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
, in which case the image is clear. The image of
\Phi|_{A_{2}}
is all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
;
I_{3}\cap R_{3}=\emptyset
; and, we do not have both
M_{4}\subseteq I_{3}
and
M_{3}\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
.

3.

Let
A_{3}\subseteq J(\mathcal{P})
be the set of
I_{1}\in J(\mathcal{P})
such that
(R_{1}\cup M_{1})\subseteq I_{1}
and
(R_{2}\cup M_{2})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
. Here, we send
M_{1}
to
M_{4}
and
R_{1}
to
\mathcal{P}_{4}
. The image of
\Phi|_{A_{3}}
is all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
;
(M_{3}\cup R_{3})\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
;
M_{4}\subset I_{3}
;
\epsilon_{1}\in I_{3}
; and,
\epsilon_{2}\notin I_{3}
.

4.

Finally, we construct a map
J(\mathcal{P}_{56})\to J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
. Given
I_{56}\in J(\mathcal{P}_{56})
, we send
I_{56}
to
(I_{56}\cup M_{4},\mathcal{P}_{4})
. The image of this map is all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
;
(M_{3}\cup R_{3})\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
;
M_{4}\subset I_{3}
; and
\epsilon_{1}\in I_{3}
only if
\epsilon_{2}\in I_{3}
.

Each described map is bijective, with clear inverse from the described image. The combinatorics of the
\mathbf{g}
-vector computation is similar to the non-intersecting case, with the roles of
\mathcal{P}(s-b-1)
and
\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+b+1)
flipped.

Case iii)
s-b=1
;
t^{\prime}+b<n
; and,
s(\gamma)
is plain.

If
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are non-intersecting, the computations are essentially the same as in case i. So we suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are intersecting. Recall from the exposition beforehand that
a>b
is the largest integer such that
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+b+1),\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+a)
correspond to a set of arcs from
T
incident to
s(\gamma)
. For shorthand, in the diagram below we denote
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(t^{\prime}+i)=\lambda_{i}
; in particular,
\lambda_{b+1}
corresponds to
\epsilon_{2}
from the exposition above the Proposition, and
\lambda_{a+1}
is not in this set of arcs with common endpoint
s(\gamma)
. It is possible that
\lambda_{a+1}
does not exist or is part of a loop. We have a diagram of
\mathcal{P}
on the left,
\mathcal{P}_{3}
in the middle, and
\mathcal{P}_{56}
on the right;
\mathcal{P}_{4}
looks the same in all our cases.

\boxed{M_{1}}
\boxed{R_{1}}
\boxed{R_{2}}
\boxed{M_{2}}
\lambda_{b+1}
\ddots
\lambda_{a}
\lambda_{a+1}
\cdots

\boxed{M_{3}}
\boxed{R_{3}}
\boxed{M_{4}}
\lambda_{b+1}
\ddots
\lambda_{a}
\lambda_{a+1}
\cdots
\lambda_{a+1}
\cdots

Subcase)
t^{\prime}+a<n

In this case,
\lambda_{a+1}
is in
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}
.
We use the same map from
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})
to
J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})
as in the general case for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
intersecting. The cokernel of the map in this case consists of all
(I_{3},I_{4})\in J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
such that
(M_{3}\cup R_{3})\cap I_{3}=\emptyset
;
I_{4}=\mathcal{P}_{4}
; and
(M_{4}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}[t^{\prime}+b+1,t^{\prime}+a])\subseteq I_{3}
. These sets are in bijection with
J(\mathcal{P}_{56})
, where we send
I_{56}
to
(I_{56}\cup(M_{4}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}[t^{\prime}+b+1,t^{\prime}+a]),\mathcal{P}_{4})
.

We check the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors in this case. It is clear that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{3}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{4}}
as in the general case, so we check only that
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{M}\prod_{i=b+1}^{a}\hat{y}_{\lambda_{i}})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{56}}
.

The expression for
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}
can be derived by taking Equation
6.2
and replacing the coefficient of
\mathbf{e}_{\beta}
with
-2\delta_{\mathcal{P}(1)\prec\mathcal{P}(2)}
, replacing the coefficient of
\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{2}}
with 1, and deleting the term
\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{1}}
. We can then replace
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime}
with
-\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{a}}+\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+a+1)\succ\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+a+2)}\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{a+1}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
where
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
reflects the contributions of elements in
\mathcal{P}[>t^{\prime}+a+1]
.

From Corollary
1
, we compute
\deg_{\bm{x}}(\prod_{i=b+1}^{a}\hat{y}_{\lambda_{i}})=-\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{2}}+\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{a}}-\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{a+1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\beta}-\mathbf{e}_{\epsilon_{1}}+\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}
where
\zeta
is the counterclockwise neighbor of
\lambda_{a}
in the triangle which is also bordered by
\lambda_{a+1}
and
\epsilon_{1}
is as in the general case. Now we have
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{56}}=\mathbf{e}_{\zeta}+(-1+\delta_{\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+a+1)\succ\mathcal{P}(t^{\prime}+a+2)})\mathbf{e}_{\lambda_{a+1}}+\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}^{\prime\prime}
and one can check
\mathbf{g}_{\gamma}+\deg_{\bm{x}}(\hat{Y}_{R}\hat{Y}_{M}\prod_{i=b+1}^{a}\hat{y}_{\lambda_{i}})=\mathbf{g}_{\gamma_{56}}
.

Subcase)

and
t(\gamma)
is plain.

If we use the same map as in the previous case, the cokernel has only one element. The poset
J(\mathcal{P}_{56})
is
\emptyset
with decoration
\{\zeta\}
. So we map the unique element of
J(\mathcal{P}_{56})
to the unique element in the cokernel of
\Phi
. The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computation is very similar to the previous.

In the special case where

. These sets are in natural bijection with

.

In the special case

, we interpret statements “

” regarding

to mean “
\epsilon_{x}
does not exist”.

Subcase)
t^{\prime}+a=n
and
t(\gamma)
is notched.

We can use the same partitioning
A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{4}=J(\mathcal{P})
and same map
\Phi:J(\mathcal{P})\to J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
. The cokernel of this map has a slightly different description, but it will again correspond bijectively to elements of
J(\mathcal{P}_{56})
.

The
\mathbf{g}
-vector computations for all of case v follows quickly from previous cases.

Case vi)
s-b>1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
.

We can switch the chronological ordering on
\mathcal{P}
to return to the
s-b=1,t^{\prime}+b<n
case.

Case vii)
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
.

If
s-b=1
and
t^{\prime}+b=n
, then
\gamma_{56}
is contractible. If
s(\gamma)
and
t(\gamma)
are both plain, then the ideas from case i give a direct bijection between
J(\mathcal{P})
and
J(\mathcal{P}_{3})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{4})
. If
s(\gamma)
and
t(\gamma)
are both notched, then we construct a similar bijection; in the special case where
(R_{1}\cup M_{1})\subseteq I_{1},(R_{2}\cup M_{2})\cap I_{1}=\emptyset
, it will be necessary to send the portion of
I_{1}
at the beginning loop and send it to the end loop in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
and similarly take the portion of
I_{1}
on the end loop and send it to the first loop in
\mathcal{P}_{3}
.

If the taggings at
s(\gamma)
and
t(\gamma)
do not agree, then
\gamma_{56}
is a contractible singly-notched monogon. The expression
x_{\gamma_{56}}
is defined in Definition
7
. Since
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
cannot be crossing in this case, we always have
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{R}Y_{M}x_{\gamma_{56}}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{R}Y_{M}(\pm 1\mp Y_{s(\gamma)})
.

Suppose
s(\gamma)
is notched and
t(\gamma)
is plain. Let the spokes at
s(\gamma)
again be
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\ldots,\epsilon_{h}
. Following the same ideas as the general case, we see there is a bijection between
J(\mathcal{P}_{1})\backslash\{M_{1}\cup R_{1}\cup\{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{h}\}\}
and
(J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{3}})\times J(\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}}))\backslash\{(M_{4},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma_{4}})\}\}
, but there is no possible image for
M_{1}\cup R_{1}\cup\{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{h}\}
, and there is no preimage to
(M_{4},\mathcal{P}_{4})
Thus, we conclude
x_{\gamma}+Y_{R}Y_{M}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{R}Y_{M}Y_{s(\gamma)}
, implying
x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{3}}x_{\gamma_{4}}+Y_{R}Y_{M}x_{\gamma_{56}}
. The case where
s(\gamma)
is plain and
t(\gamma)
is notched follows from a similar, dual argument.
∎

7
Closed Curves

We consider intersections involving a closed curve. The presence of punctures does not affect the intersection of two closed curves, so these can be computed using methods from
[
25
]
or
[
7
]
.

Intersections between a closed curve
\xi
and an arc
\gamma
can be characterized into Type 0 and Type 1, as in Section
5
based on whether there exists a representative from the isotopy class of
\gamma
such that the point of intersection lies in the first or last triangle
\gamma
passes through. There is no analogue for a Type 2 crossing since
\xi
does not have a well-defined first or last triangle it passes through.

When the intersection is of Type 0, we orient
\xi
so that in the region corresponding to the crossing overlap, the orientation of
\gamma
and
\xi
agree. When the intersection is of Type 1, suppose the point of intersection is in the first triangle
\gamma
passes through, and orient
\xi
so that near the point of intersection,
s(\gamma)
lies to its right. Then, we label the resolution of the intersection between
\xi
and
\gamma
by
\{\gamma_{34}\}\cup\{\gamma_{56}\}
where
\gamma_{34}=\gamma\circ\xi\circ\gamma
and
\gamma_{56}=\gamma\circ\xi^{-1}\circ\gamma
. This closely resembles the resolution
\{\gamma_{3},\gamma_{4}\}\cup\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{6}\}
for Type 0 and 1 intersections of a pair of arcs respectively.

Proposition 18
.

Let
\xi
be a closed curve and let
\gamma
be an arc.

1.

Suppose
\xi
and
\gamma
intersect in a Type 0 crossing and let
R_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\xi}[s,t]
and
R_{2}=\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]
be the corresponding crossing overlap in the posets. Then,

x_{\xi}x_{\gamma}=x_{\gamma_{34}}+Y_{R}Y_{S_{s}}Y_{S_{t}}x_{\gamma_{56}}

where
Y_{R}=\prod_{i=s}^{t}y_{P_{\xi}(i)}
,

Y_{S_{s}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(s-1)\\
i\leq s-1\end{subarray}}&s^{\prime}=1;s(\gamma)\text{ is notched; and, }\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\text{ is on bottom}\\
\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(s-1)\\
i\leq s-1\end{subarray}}&s^{\prime}=1;s(\gamma)\text{ is plain; and, }\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\text{ is on top}\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and for
n=|\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}^{0}|
,

Y_{S_{t}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(t+1)\\
i\geq t+1\end{subarray}}&t^{\prime}=d;t(\gamma)\text{ is notched; and, }\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\text{ is on bottom}\\
\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(t+1)\\
i\geq t+1\end{subarray}}&t^{\prime}=d;t(\gamma)\text{ is plain; and, }\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}[s^{\prime},t^{\prime}]\text{ is on top}\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

2.

Suppose
\xi
and
\gamma
intersect in a Type 1 crossing near
s(\gamma)
. Let
i
be such that this point of intersection occurs between the crossings between
\xi
and
T
corresponding to
\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i+1)
. Then,

x_{\xi}x_{\gamma}=Y_{\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\xi(i)}}x_{\gamma_{34}}+x_{\gamma_{56}}

and

x_{\xi}x_{\gamma^{(s(\gamma))}}=x_{\gamma_{34}}+Y_{\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\xi(i+1)}}x_{\gamma_{56}}

where
Y_{\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\xi(i)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(j)\preceq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(j)}
and
Y_{\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\xi(i+1)}}=\prod_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(j)\succeq\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i+1)}y_{\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(j)}
.

The proof of part 1 follows from the proof of Proposition
6
while part 2 follows from Proposition
7
. By our assumption about the orientation of
\xi
, in part 2
\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i)\prec\mathcal{P}_{\xi}(i+1)
.

8
Implications

8.1
Main Theorem

We have now shown Theorem
1
by showing it is true for any possible pair of incompatible curves as well as for any self-incompatible curve. One can compare our list of cases with the list found in Section 8.4 of
[
6
]
to show that all possible configurations are discussed. Moreover, in each case, one can see that the description of the
y
-monomial matches the more general description provided in the introduction. In the unpunctured case, our description matches that of
[
7
]
.

8.2
Bangles and Bracelets

Having explicit skein relations for punctured surfaces has immediate corollaries regarding the analogues of the bracelet and bangles bases from
[
6
]
. We first recall the definitions of
bangles
and
bracelets
.

Definition 8

(Definition 3.16,
[
6
]
)
.

Given an essential loop
\xi
on
(S,M)
, the
bangle

\textrm{Bang}_{k}{\xi}
is the union of
k
loops that are isotopic to
\xi
, with no self-crossings.The
bracelet

\textrm{Brac}_{k}{\xi}
is constructed by concatenating
\xi
exactly
k
times, producing a closed loop with
k-1
self-crossings.

Definition 9

(Definition 8.2
[
6
]
)
.

•

A collection
C
of tagged arcs and essential loops on
(S,M)
is called
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
-compatible
if its tagged arcs are pairwise compatible. Let
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}(S,M)
to denote the set of all
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
-compatible collections on
(S,M)
.

•

A collection
C
of tagged arcs and bracelets is
C
-compatible
if its tagged arcs are pairwise compatible; no two elements of
C
cross each other except for self-crossings of bracelets; and, for any essential loop
\gamma
in
(S,M)
, there exists at most one
k\geq 1
such that
\textrm{Brac}_{k}\gamma
lies in
C
(and there is at most one copy of this bracelet in
C
).

From sets of
\mathcal{C}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{C}
-compatible curves, we define two sets of monomials in the cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
associated to
(S,M)
. The sets
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
are then defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\prod_{c\in C}x_{c}:C\in\mathcal{C}^{\circ}(S,M)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\prod_{c\in C}x_{c}:C\in\mathcal{C}(S,M)\right\}.

In the case of an unpunctured surface (in which case one omits the adjective “tagged” in the description), these two sets of monomials are shown to be bases of
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
in Theorem 1.1 of
[
6
]
. The basis
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is called the bangles basis and
\mathcal{B}
is called the bracelet basis.

Having skein relations allows us to quickly make progress towards showing that these sets also provide bases for a cluster algebra from a punctured surface. The most immediate corollary shows that these sets span
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
.

Corollary 4
.

Given a possibly punctured surface
(S,M)
, the sets
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
span
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
.

From Theorem
1
, the proof of Corollary
4
follows the proof of the result in the unpunctured case, which appears as Lemma 4.7 in
[
6
]
. The relationship between bracelets and bangles via Chebyshev polynomials, given in Proposition 4.2, of the same article still holds in a punctured surface.

We have a second immediate corollary, which results from the description of the
y
-monomial appearing in the skein relations. This result would be a first step towards showing linear independence of
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
if one were to follow the same strategy as in
[
6
]
.

Corollary 5
.

Lemma 6.3 from
[
6
]
holds for a punctured surface as well.

Proof.

We can follow the same proof strategy as for the original lemma, combining the following two observations. From Theorem
1
, we know that each skein relation has one term without a
y
-monomial. The homogeneity of elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
follows in the punctured case can be shown in the same way as in the unpunctured case using Wilson’s lattice structure on good matchings of loop graphs
[
5
]
.
∎

Armed with Corollary
5
, it is straightforward to follow the recipe in Section 8.5 of
[
6
]
to show that the elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
are linearly independent and similarly for
\mathcal{B}
. This argument uses skein relations to show that we can realize the
\mathbf{g}
-vectors as giving an injection from
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
or
\mathcal{B}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
; if the surface is not once-punctured and closed, this injection will in fact be a bijection. If we have a surface with at least two marked points on the boundary or a closed surface of genus 0, we can then use the suggestions in Section 8.6 of the same article to show that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
are both subsets of
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
. Recall in the latter setting, we require there to be at least four marked points, as is standard. Combining these suggestions with Corollary
4
allows us to conclude that
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
are bases in such settings.

If our surface has only one marked point on the boundary, we must use another method. Recall that the
upper cluster algebra

\mathcal{U}
of a cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}
consists of all elements which can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial in any cluster of the cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}
. The snake graph construction ensures that all elements of
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{B}
are indeed in
\mathcal{U}(S,M)
. Proposition 12 of
[
45
]
, shows that, if
(S,M)
is a surface with exactly one marked point on the boundary, then
\mathcal{A}(S,M)=\mathcal{U}(S,M)
. Therefore, we can again conclude
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{A}(S,M)
and
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}(S,M)
. With these ingredients, we can that each set is indeed a basis in a wide array of settings.

Theorem 2
.

Let
(S,M)
be such that
S
has a non-empty boundary, or
S
has genus 0 and
|M|>3
. Then,
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
is a basis for
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
and similarly,
\mathcal{B}
is a basis for
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
.

As mentioned in the introduction, this general statement of the theorem includes some cases where the result was already known. The fact that the bracelets
\mathcal{B}
form bases for surfaces with at least two marked points on the boundary follows from results of Mandel-Qin
[
9
]
and Muller
[
31
]
. Geiß, Labardini-Fragoso, and Wilson
[
20
]
showed that the bangles
\mathcal{B}^{\circ}
form bases in the coefficient-free case for surfaces with non-empty boundary.

The only obstruction to extending the main result of
[
6
]
to surfaces not covered in the hypothesis of Theorem
2
is showing that the expressions associated to essential loops are elements of
\mathcal{A}(S,M)
when
(S,M)
is closed and has nonzero genus. For example, recall that the Markov cluster algebra is the cluster algebra associated to a once-punctured torus. Bernstein, Fomin, and Zelevinsky show that the Markov cluster algebra is properly contained in its upper cluster algebra by exhibiting a specific element in the complement
[
46
]
. This proof can be slightly modified to show that the bracelet function (equivalently, bangle function) associated to a certain essential loop on the torus does not lie in the cluster algebra; as previously noted, it will lie in the upper cluster algebra.

We note that in general cluster algebras from closed surfaces of positive genus are known to differ from their upper cluster algebras (shown in the once-punctured case by Ladkani
[
47
]
and by Moon and Wong for a general number of punctures
[
48
]
). Conversely, for surfaces with boundary, Theorem
2
applies to all cases where the cluster algebra equals its upper cluster algebra (for proofs see
[
26
,
49
]
).

8.3
Punctured Orbifolds

Analogous to cluster algebras of surface type, a subset of Chekhov and Shapiro’s
generalized cluster algebras
arise from triangulated orbifolds
[
50
]
. In fact, the definition of a generalized cluster algebra was inspired by how lambda lengths in an orbifold mutate. In
[
32
]
, the first and third authors extended the snake graph construction of Musiker, Schiffler, and Williams for generalized cluster algebras from
unpunctured
orbifolds and further follow
[
7
]
to provide matrix formulas and skein relations in this setting.

Our study of skein relations for punctured orbifolds was originally motivated by a desire to extend the generalized snake graphs of
[
32
]
to the punctured setting. While ordinary tagged arcs on a triangulated orbifold behave similarly to the surface case, generalized tagged arcs which wind nontrivially around an orbifold point require new and intricate constructions. Proving that the snake graphs corresponding to generalized arcs produce “sensible” expansions in the generalized cluster algebra requires an understanding of skein relations in the presence of punctures.

In upcoming work, we will collaborate with the authors of
[
12
]
to use the proof methods demonstrated in this paper to generalize their poset expansion formulae to the orbifold setting. This work will also allow us to give matrix formulae for generalized cluster algebras in the
[
15
]
, which we emphasize are distinct from those found in
[
32
]
.

*
